Prosthesis–patient mismatch, aortic

### Imaging the prosthesis

The figure shows a 23-mm stented bovine pericardial valve or a 23-mm bileaflet mechanical valve; the short axis shows the EOA as the area of the bioprosthesis's orifice. Reverberation and shadowing from an aortic prosthesis prevent a complete assessment of the posterior annulus and root on TTE, and can limit the anterior root on TEE; TEE is recommended when the posterior annulus or root is poorly seen on TTE, or may be abnormal[71](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-71).

An initial TTE after implantation is recommended[38](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-38); this baseline study shows whether mismatch is present, and later studies are compared with it[71](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-71). After a surgical bioprosthesis, TTE at 5 and 10 years and then annually is reasonable, even without a change in clinical status[38](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-38).

### Gradients, EOA and DVI

The pressure gradient is 4V², or 4(V2² − V1²) when the LVOT velocity exceeds 1.5 m/s[71](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-71). The EOA by the continuity equation ( [chapter 12](https://intro2echo.com/as)) depends less on flow than the gradient does[71](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-71), and it is compared with the normal values of the same valve type and size[71](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-71). The LVOT diameter is the largest diameter square to the LVOT, just below the valve plane, not the label size of the prosthesis[71](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-71).

The DVI, the LVOT VTI divided by the VTI of the jet, needs no LVOT diameter; after surgical aortic valve replacement, a DVI of 0.35 or less is associated with adverse outcomes[71](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-71).

In a bileaflet mechanical valve, CW Doppler records the faster jet of the narrow central orifice, and the pressure recovers past the valve ( [chapter 07](https://intro2echo.com/pressure)): the gradient is overestimated and the EOA underestimated, most in small valves at high flow[71](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-71). The normal values of each valve include this effect[71](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-71).

### Grading prosthetic aortic stenosis

As a prosthesis narrows, its jet peaks later, and the contour turns from triangular to rounded and symmetric[71](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-71). The acceleration time, from the onset of flow to the peak velocity, is normal below 80 ms and suggests significant stenosis above 100 ms, and its ratio to the ejection time (AT/ET) is normal below 0.32 and suggests significant stenosis above 0.37. LV function and heart rate also change the contour and the acceleration time[71](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-71).

Significant stenosis should meet at least one flow-dependent measure (the peak velocity or the mean gradient) and one flow-independent measure (the EOA or the DVI)[71](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-71). Prosthetic valve obstruction is usually defined as a rise in the mean gradient of more than 50% from baseline, or of more than 10 mmHg across an aortic prosthesis, once other causes such as a high-output state are excluded[38](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-38). With poor LV function or a high blood pressure, the gradient can stay low despite significant stenosis[71](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-71).

The figure grades possible stenosis by any measure outside its normal column, and significant stenosis by this rule; it takes a DVI above 0.35 as normal, by the table, while the guideline's algorithm for a high velocity separates its branches at 0.30 and 0.25.

### Obstruction, mismatch or high flow

In prosthesis–patient mismatch, the EOA of a normal prosthesis is too small for the patient's body size and flow needs: the indexed EOA (the EOA divided by the body surface area) is small[71](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-71), and 0.85 cm²/m² or less is the usual threshold in the aortic position[72](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-72). With a BMI below 30 kg/m², mismatch is moderate at 0.66–0.85 cm²/m² and severe at 0.65 cm²/m² or less[71](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-71). The indexed EOA overestimates mismatch in obesity, so with a BMI of 30 kg/m² or more the cut-offs are 0.70 and 0.55 cm²/m²[71](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-71).

High-flow states, mismatch, obstruction above or below the valve and pressure recovery should be excluded as causes of a high gradient; the velocity cut-offs of the table hold for a normal stroke volume, 50–90 mL[71](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-71). For a peak velocity above 3 m/s, the ASE algorithm reads the contour and the DVI. An early peak (acceleration time below 100 ms, AT/ET below 0.37) with a DVI of 0.30 or more indicates a normal prosthesis: an indexed EOA below 0.85 cm²/m² then points to mismatch, and one above it to a high-flow state. A DVI of 0.25–0.29 indicates possible stenosis, and a late peak with a DVI below 0.25 indicates stenosis[71](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-71).

With normal leaflet motion and no thrombus, a high gradient means mismatch, pannus, or both[38](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-38). In the figure, mismatch is the same valve, opening normally, in patients with a larger body surface area and the same stroke volume index.

### TEE, fluoroscopy and CT

When symptoms or signs suggest prosthetic dysfunction, TEE, gated cardiac CT or fluoroscopy is recommended, even if TTE shows no dysfunction[38](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-38). Strong reflections obscure the motion of mechanical leaflets on TTE in most patients, and fluoroscopy or cine-CT is recommended to assess it[38](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-38); on CT, a bileaflet valve normally opens to 73–90°[71](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-71).

Contrast CT can separate thrombus from pannus: thrombus is more common early after surgery, usually lies on the aortic side and measures less than 200 HU; pannus forms late, usually on the ventricular side, and measures more than 200 HU[71](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-71). In a stenotic bioprosthesis, a thin layer of thrombus on the cusps can cause the obstruction and can resolve with oral anticoagulation; 3D TEE or 4D CT can detect it[38](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-38).

### Paravalvular regurgitation

In a surgical valve, a paravalvular leak arises from dehiscence of the sewing ring, and its jet arises and runs outside the ring[71](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-71). The share of the ring's circumference that the jets occupy grades the leak, together with the vena contracta: less than 10% is mild, 10–29% moderate and 30% or more severe[71](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-71). A normal bileaflet valve has small washing jets inside the sewing ring and in the centre[71](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-71).

### Key points

1. The EOA is compared with the reference of the valve's type and size; the LVOT diameter is measured, not taken from the label size.
2. Significant stenosis needs a flow-dependent measure (peak velocity ≥4 m/s or mean gradient ≥35 mmHg) and a flow-independent one (DVI <0.25 or EOA 2 SDs below the reference).
3. A late-peaking, rounded jet with an acceleration time above 100 ms and a low DVI suggests stenosis; an early peak, a normal DVI and a small indexed EOA suggest mismatch.
4. Fluoroscopy or CT shows the motion of mechanical leaflets, and CT separates thrombus from pannus.

B7

## Infective endocarditis

Vegetations, leaflet perforation and the echocardiographic criteria

Echocardiography shows the lesions of infective endocarditis: the vegetation, and the destruction that it causes, such as leaflet perforation and abscess. The size and mobility of a vegetation predict embolism, and a length of 10 mm is the threshold in the ESC recommendations for surgery.

Severity vegetation 14 mm

none<10 mm≥10 mmmild MRmoderate MRsevere MR

Frame

CineSystoleDiastole

Vegetation14mm

Mobilityprolapsing

Vena contractanone

Mitral regurgitationnone

**Vegetation of 10 mm or more.** A 14-mm mass is attached to the atrial side of the anterior mitral leaflet, near its line of closure, with the gray scale of myocardium[44](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-44). It is pedunculated and prolapses across the coaptation plane, into the LA in systole and into the LV in diastole: a highly mobile vegetation[44](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-44). Its maximal length is 10 mm or more: with another indication for surgery, urgent surgery is recommended (ESC class I), and without severe valve dysfunction or clinical embolism it may be considered at low surgical risk (class IIb)[74](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-74).

### The echocardiographic criteria

In the 2023 Duke-ISCVID criteria, echocardiography or cardiac CT that shows a vegetation, a valvular or leaflet perforation, a valvular or leaflet aneurysm, an abscess, a pseudoaneurysm or an intracardiac fistula is a major criterion[73](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-73). Significant new valvular regurgitation on echocardiography and a new partial dehiscence of a prosthetic valve, compared with previous imaging, are major criteria too; worsening or a change of preexisting regurgitation is not sufficient[73](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-73). The 2023 ESC criteria count the lesions characteristic of IE on echocardiography, cardiac CT, \[18F\]FDG-PET/CT or WBC SPECT/CT as one major imaging criterion[74](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-74).

The ESC defined the lesions on echocardiography as follows[75](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-75):

- **Vegetation.** An oscillating or non-oscillating intracardiac mass on a valve or another endocardial structure, or on implanted intracardiac material.
- **Perforation.** An interruption of endocardial tissue continuity traversed by color Doppler flow.
- **Abscess.** A thickened, non-homogeneous perivalvular area with an echodense or echolucent appearance.
- **Pseudoaneurysm.** A pulsatile perivalvular echo-free space with flow on color Doppler.
- **Fistula.** Color Doppler communication between two neighboring cavities through a perforation.
- **Valve aneurysm.** A saccular bulging of valvular tissue.

### TTE and TOE

TTE is recommended as the first-line imaging modality in suspected IE[74](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-74). TOE is recommended when TTE is negative or nondiagnostic and the clinical suspicion of IE remains, in suspected IE with a prosthetic valve or an intracardiac device, and after a positive TTE, except in isolated right-sided native valve IE with a TTE of good quality and unequivocal findings[74](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-74). When the first examination is negative or inconclusive and the suspicion remains high, repeating TTE or TOE within 5–7 days is recommended[74](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-74).

TTE has limits for perivalvular complications, small vegetations, prosthetic valves and device leads[74](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-74). For leaflet perforation, the estimated sensitivity is 45% for TTE and 95% for TOE, with a specificity of 98% for both[44](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-44).

### The vegetation

A vegetation lies typically on the atrial side of an atrioventricular valve or on the ventricular side of the aortic valve[44](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-44). It is usually amorphous, shaggy or lobulated, with the echo texture of myocardium; a healed vegetation is brighter and often calcified[44](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-44). It moves with a high-frequency flutter, independent of the motion of the valve[44](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-44). The size of a vegetation is its maximal length[74](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-74).

### Size, mobility and embolism

Embolic events affect 20–50% of patients with IE, and they may be clinically silent in up to 50% of patients[74](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-74). The risk is highest in the days around the diagnosis and falls within the first 2 weeks of antibiotic therapy[74](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-74). The size and mobility of the vegetation are the most important independent predictors of a new embolic event; a location on the mitral valve, a change of size under antibiotic therapy and _S. aureus_ also raise the risk[74](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-74). The greatest risk appears to be with vegetations of 10 mm or more on the anterior mitral leaflet[76](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-76), and the risk of neurological complications is particularly high with vegetations longer than 30 mm[74](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-74).

In the studies of embolic risk, a highly mobile vegetation was pedunculated and prolapsed across the coaptation plane: a mitral vegetation prolapses into the LV in diastole and into the LA in systole[44](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-44).

### Surgery to prevent embolism

The ESC recommendations use a vegetation length of 10 mm[74](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-74):

- Urgent surgery is recommended in aortic or mitral IE with a persistent vegetation of 10 mm or more after one or more embolic episodes despite appropriate antibiotic therapy (class I).
- Urgent surgery is recommended in IE with a vegetation of 10 mm or more and another indication for surgery (class I).
- Urgent surgery may be considered in aortic or mitral IE with a vegetation of 10 mm or more, without severe valve dysfunction or clinical evidence of embolism, at low surgical risk (class IIb)[74](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-74).

Urgent surgery is surgery within 3–5 days[74](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-74). The AHA statement uses mobile vegetations longer than 10 mm: in native valve IE, early surgery is reasonable with such a vegetation and severe valve regurgitation (class IIa)[76](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-76).

### Perforation and acute regurgitation

Leaflet perforation, leaflet rupture and mitral chordal rupture cause new severe regurgitation, or worsen regurgitation already present, with acute heart failure[74](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-74). Urgent surgery is recommended in aortic or mitral IE with severe acute regurgitation that causes symptoms of heart failure or echocardiographic signs of poor hemodynamic tolerance (class I), and emergency surgery, within 24 h, when it causes refractory pulmonary edema or cardiogenic shock (class I)[74](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-74).

In acute MR, the low blood pressure and the high LA pressure lower the driving pressure, so the jet is slower; it is usually markedly eccentric, so color Doppler can underestimate the MR[29](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-29). Systolic flow reversal in the pulmonary veins is usually present[29](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-29) ( [bonus chapter B10](https://intro2echo.com/mi)). The guideline's Doppler methods for the severity of MR apply to chronic, not to acute severe MR, and TOE may identify acute severe MR better[29](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-29). The figure grades the perforation jet by its vena contracta with the cut-offs of chronic MR: below 0.3 cm mild, and 0.7 cm or more severe[29](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-29) ( [chapter 15](https://intro2echo.com/mr)).

### Differential diagnosis

Echocardiography should distinguish the vegetation of IE from a non-bacterial thrombotic vegetation and benign intracardiac masses[74](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-74). Lambl excrescences, which were not related to systemic embolism in a TEE study, and the papillary fibroelastoma are described in [bonus chapter B9](https://intro2echo.com/masses); on a valve, a fibroelastoma lies typically on the aortic side of the aortic valve or on the ventricular side of the mitral valve, the opposite of the typical location of IE[44](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-44). Libman–Sacks vegetations, the non-bacterial thrombotic vegetations of systemic lupus erythematosus and the antiphospholipid syndrome, are rarely associated with valve dysfunction and never with valve perforation[74](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-74). Previous scarring and severe myxomatous change can be indistinguishable from active lesions[76](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-76).

### Key points

1. A vegetation, perforation, aneurysm, abscess, pseudoaneurysm or fistula on echocardiography or CT is a major Duke-ISCVID criterion[73](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-73).
2. TOE is recommended when TTE is negative or nondiagnostic and suspicion remains, with a prosthetic valve or a device, and after a positive TTE, except in isolated right-sided native valve IE with a clear TTE[74](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-74).
3. Size, the maximal length, and mobility best predict embolism; 10 mm is the length in the ESC recommendations for urgent surgery[74](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-74).
4. In acute MR from a perforation, color Doppler can underestimate the eccentric jet; systolic flow reversal in the pulmonary veins supports severe MR[29](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-29).

18

## Transcatheter aortic valve replacement

TAVR, step by step: what TEE shows at each step

Transcatheter aortic valve replacement (TAVR) places a valve on a metal frame inside the stenotic aortic valve, brought on a catheter from the femoral artery. Fluoroscopy guides the procedure, TEE helps at each step, and TTE shows the result.

Step Before: TTE

Before: TTEMeasureCrossDeployCheckAfter: TTE

Valve

Balloon-expandableSelf-expanding

Leak

NoneMildModerateSevere

Step1 of 6

Aortic stenosissevere

Mean gradient47mmHg

**Before: TTE.** The parasternal long and short axes show a calcified aortic valve that opens little. From the apex, CW records a peak velocity of 4.4 m/s and a mean gradient of 47 mmHg: severe aortic stenosis[30](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-30).

| Parameter | Mild | Moderate | Severe |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Circumferential extent of the jets | <10% | 10–29% | ≥30% |
| Vena contracta width | <0.3 cm | 0.3–0.6 cm | >0.6 cm |
| Jet density, CW | Incomplete or faint | Dense | Dense |
| Pressure half-time, CW | >500 ms | 200–500 ms | <200 ms |
| Diastolic flow reversal, descending aorta | Absent or brief, early | Intermediate | Prominent, holodiastolic |
| Regurgitant volume | <30 mL | 30–59 mL | ≥60 mL |
| Regurgitant fraction | <30% | 30–50% | ≥50% |

Paravalvular regurgitation after TAVR

### The device

A bioprosthesis on a metal frame is crimped onto a catheter and expanded inside the stenotic native valve. A balloon-expandable valve, the SAPIEN 3 (a cobalt-chromium frame with bovine pericardial leaflets), opens with one balloon inflation under rapid ventricular pacing[77](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-77). A self-expanding valve, the Evolut (a nitinol frame with porcine pericardial leaflets[78](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-78) that sit above the annulus[79](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-79)), is released slowly from its capsule without rapid pacing[80](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-80), and can be recaptured before its release[78](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-78). From 2019 to 2022, 96.2% of TAVRs in the US registry used transfemoral access[81](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-81). The figure shows a 26-mm SAPIEN 3.

### Who is treated

TAVR lowered the 1-year mortality of patients who could not have surgery from 50.7% with standard therapy to 30.7%[82](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-82), and the 1-year death, stroke or rehospitalization of patients at low surgical risk from 15.1% after surgery to 8.5%[83](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-83). The 2025 ESC/EACTS guideline recommends TAVR from 70 years of age with a tricuspid valve and suitable anatomy, and surgery below 70 at low surgical risk[84](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-84).

### The anatomy for TAVR

The annulus that sizes the valve is the diameter at the hinge points of the cusps, measured in systole[85](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-85): from the right cusp's hinge anteriorly to the base of the interleaflet trigone posteriorly, inner edge to inner edge, on a zoomed long axis[86](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-86). A TEE annulus of 21–25 mm takes a 26-mm SAPIEN 3[77](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-77), and an annulus of 23–26 mm a 29-mm Evolut R[78](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-78). CT is the reference for sizing; 3D TEE measures 3.5–7.4% smaller, usually without a change of valve size[87](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-87). A left main height below 10–11 mm warns of coronary obstruction[86](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-86).

### The steps on TEE

Fluoroscopy guides most of the procedure; TEE or TTE helps to place the pacing wire, the guidewire and the valve, and checks the result at once[88](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-88). TEE gives continuous images, but needs sedation or anesthesia, and its probe can obstruct fluoroscopy[80](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-80). Conscious sedation for transfemoral TAVR rose from 33.4% to 64.1% of US cases between 2016 and 2019[89](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-89). The EACVI prefers this minimalist approach with TTE, converting to TEE for complications; TEE suits new programs and patients at higher risk[87](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-87). The views below follow EACVI 2023[87](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-87) ( [chapter 17](https://intro2echo.com/tee)).

1. **Measure.** The midesophageal long axis of the aortic valve, at 120–140°, and its short axis, at 30–60°, are often acquired as a biplane image[87](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-87). The long axis gives the annulus, the short axis the cusps and their calcium, and a 3D zoom of the root the annular area, its perimeter and the left main height[86](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-86). The deep transgastric view aligns the Doppler beam with the valve for the baseline gradient[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90).
2. **Cross.** TEE confirms the pacing wire in the RV[87](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-87) and, on the mitral biplane views, watches the stiff wire in the LV for entanglement in the mitral apparatus, its commonest complication: a new change in the mitral leaflets or in MR[91](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-91). Echo places the crimped valve by the hinge points of the cusps, which can lie slightly aortic to the fluoroscopic landmark[91](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-91).
3. **Deploy.** A SAPIEN 3 is placed for a final depth of 80/20 to 90/10 of its frame height, aortic to ventricular[92](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-92). Its frame shortens as it expands, mostly at its ventricular end: from 27 to 20 mm for the 26-mm valve[93](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-93). With the cusp-overlap technique, an Evolut targets 3 mm below the noncoronary cusp, and is recaptured at less than 1 mm or more than 5 mm[94](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-94).
4. **Check.** Right after the implant, TEE confirms that all the prosthetic cusps move, that the frame is circular and that there is no significant valvular or paravalvular regurgitation; central regurgitation is common while the wire crosses the valve and for minutes after, as the crimped leaflets recover. Transgastric views with color, PW and CW Doppler complete the check before the probe is removed[85](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-85).

The whole valve is scanned from its aortic to its ventricular end. A paravalvular jet arises at the frame's ventricular edge and counts only when it enters the LV; color in the sinuses above the skirt is not a leak[80](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-80). Its circumferential extent is the sum of the jets' vena contracta arcs divided by the circumference of the valve's outer edge: less than 10% is mild, 10–29% moderate and 30% or more severe, best read with the vena contracta[71](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-71). The frame shadows posterior jets on TTE and anterior jets on TEE; the deep transgastric view finds anterior jets that the esophageal views miss. The regurgitant volume and fraction cannot be measured during the procedure[80](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-80). With the SAPIEN 3, at least moderate paravalvular regurgitation at 30 days carried a hazard ratio of 2.40 for death at 1 year, and mild regurgitation did not raise mortality; at least moderate regurgitation is corrected at the procedure, by balloon post-dilatation for example[95](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-95).

### After the procedure

A TTE soon after the implant sets the baseline[80](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-80). VARC-3 defines the intended performance of the valve as a mean gradient below 20 mmHg, a peak velocity below 3 m/s, a DVI of 0.25 or more and less than moderate regurgitation[96](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-96). For the EOA, the LVOT diameter is measured outer edge to outer edge at the ventricular end of the frame, with the PW sample just proximal to the flow acceleration at the frame's inlet; a sample inside the frame overestimates the EOA[71](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-71). The normal values depend on the valve and its size: a mean gradient of 10.6 mmHg and an EOA of 1.74 cm² for a 26-mm SAPIEN 3, and 7.9 mmHg and 1.97 cm² for a 29-mm Evolut R[71](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-71); mismatch is graded from the indexed EOA, as after surgery ( [bonus chapter B6](https://intro2echo.com/prosthetic)). Later, a mean gradient that rises by 10 mmHg or more from the study at 1–3 months, to 20 mmHg or more, with a fall of the EOA or the DVI, is moderate hemodynamic valve deterioration[96](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-96).

A periaortic hematoma, a fast-growing pericardial effusion and atypical flow from the annulus indicate annular rupture[91](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-91). A new regional wall-motion abnormality suggests coronary obstruction[87](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-87). New MR can come from RV pacing, from a low valve that presses on the anterior mitral leaflet, or from damage to the subvalvular apparatus[85](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-85). A perforation of the RV by the pacing wire can appear only after the wire's removal[91](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-91).

### Key points

1. The annulus is measured in systole at the cusps' hinge points, inner edge to inner edge[85](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-85), [86](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-86).
2. A SAPIEN 3 is placed for a final depth of 80/20 to 90/10; its frame shortens mostly at its ventricular end[92](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-92), [93](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-93).
3. A paravalvular jet counts where it enters the LVOT; an extent below 10% of the circumference is mild, 10–29% moderate and 30% or more severe[80](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-80), [71](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-71).
4. Intended performance: a mean gradient below 20 mmHg, a peak velocity below 3 m/s, a DVI of 0.25 or more and less than moderate regurgitation[96](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-96).

19

## Mitral transcatheter edge-to-edge repair

M-TEER, step by step: what TEE shows at each step

Mitral transcatheter edge-to-edge repair (M-TEER) joins the anterior and posterior mitral leaflets with a clip, brought from the femoral vein across the atrial septum. TEE guides each step of the procedure, and TTE shows the result.

Step Before: TTE

Before: TTETransseptal punctureSteeringAlignmentGraspingAssessmentReleaseAfter: TTE

Step1 of 8

Mitral regurgitationsevere

Vena contracta0.85cm

**Severe primary MR from a flail P2.** The free edge of P2 turns into the LA in systole, and an eccentric jet runs away from it, along the anterior wall of the LA. Vena contracta 0.85 cm, EROA 0.57 cm²[29](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-29).

### The device

Two devices are approved, each with its own implantation system[97](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-97). A steerable guide catheter enters the femoral vein and runs up the inferior vena cava to the right atrium[98](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-98); over a wire it crosses the septum, and about 2 cm of it lies in the LA[97](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-97). The delivery system goes through the guide into the LA and carries the implant[97](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-97). The implant has two arms (paddles in one device), with a gripper (a clasp) over each, and the grippers drop together or one at a time[97](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-97). Each device comes in more than one size: a longer one for a larger gap, a shorter and narrower one for a grasp away from the middle of the valve[97](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-97).

### Who is treated

EVEREST II randomly assigned 279 patients with grade 3+ or 4+ MR to TEER or to surgery[99](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-99); most had primary MR[98](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-98). At 12 months, freedom from death, from surgery for mitral dysfunction and from grade 3+ or 4+ MR was 55% after TEER and 73% after surgery; major adverse events at 30 days were 15% and 48%[99](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-99). The FDA approved the first mitral TEER device in 2013 for primary MR at prohibitive surgical risk[98](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-98), and for secondary MR in 2019[100](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-100).

In secondary MR, COAPT (614 patients) lowered the rate of heart failure hospitalization within 24 months, 35.8% against 67.9% per patient-year, and death, 29.1% against 46.1%[101](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-101). MITRA-FR (304 patients) found no difference in death or heart failure hospitalization at 12 months[102](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-102). RESHAPE-HF2 (505 patients, moderate to severe secondary MR) lowered the rate of heart failure hospitalization or cardiovascular death, 37.0 against 58.9 events per 100 patient-years[103](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-103).

The 2020 ACC/AHA guideline finds TEER reasonable in severely symptomatic (NYHA III–IV) patients with severe primary MR at high or prohibitive surgical risk, with favorable anatomy and a life expectancy of at least 1 year; and in chronic severe secondary MR with persistent symptoms on optimal therapy, an anatomy suitable on TEE, an LVEF of 20–50%, an LVESD of 70 mm or less and a PA systolic pressure of 70 mmHg or less[38](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-38). In the 2025 ESC/EACTS guidelines, TEER should be considered in symptomatic severe primary MR with a suitable anatomy at high surgical risk, and it is recommended in severe ventricular secondary MR that meets their clinical and echocardiographic criteria[84](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-84).

### The anatomy for TEER

In EVEREST, the jet came from A2–P2; a valve area under 4.0 cm² excluded the patient; in primary MR the flail gap was under 10 mm and the flail width under 15 mm, and in secondary MR the coaptation length was at least 2 mm and its depth 11 mm or less[85](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-85). A baseline valve area of 3.0 cm² or less with a mean gradient of 4 mmHg or more carried a high risk of a procedure stopped for stenosis[80](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-80).

### The steps on TEE

1. **Transseptal puncture.** The sheath tents the fossa ovalis. The bicaval view places the tent between the venae cavae, the short-axis view at the aortic valve to the front or back, and the four-chamber view gives its height above the point where the clip will grasp: about 4.5–5.0 cm[97](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-97). With a flail or prolapse, that point lies at or a little above the annulus[97](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-97). The needle goes through the posterior-mid fossa, in a posterior and superior direction[85](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-85).
2. **Steering and alignment.** The clip goes down over the jet's origin and stops about 1 cm above the valve[85](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-85), [97](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-97). The commissural view (55–75°) shows its medial-lateral position, and the long-axis view (100–160°) its anterior-posterior position[85](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-85). With the arms open, the clip turns until they are square to the line of coaptation[97](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-97). The arms then lie in the long-axis plane: that view shows both, and the commissural view shows them edge-on, as one line. 3D en face and transgastric views show the turn[85](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-85).
3. **Grasping.** The clip crosses below the leaflet tips with its arms open or closed; there the arms open, and the clip comes back until the leaflets lie on them[97](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-97). The grippers drop onto the arms, together or one at a time, and the clip closes[97](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-97). The leaflets drape over the arms, the grasped tissue stops moving, and 3D shows a tissue bridge[97](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-97). Leaflet length from the annulus, before and after the grasp, estimates how much leaflet the clip holds[97](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-97).
4. **Assessment.** With the settings used before the clip, the commissural view shows the jets on both sides of it[80](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-80). Two or three thin jets are usually no more than mild MR, and PISA is not recommended after TEER[80](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-80), [97](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-97). A mean gradient under 5 mmHg with a valve area of 2.0 cm² or more is preferred, reported with the heart rate[97](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-97). In 268 patients, most with secondary MR, an invasive gradient above 5 mmHg just after implantation predicted a worse long-term outcome[104](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-104).
5. **Release.** Before release, the MR reduction is weighed against the rise in gradient[97](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-97). The clip is then detached from the delivery system[85](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-85). After release it often moves 1–2 mm toward the LV, and it can turn a little[97](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-97).

The 3D en face view from the LA shows the valve as the surgeon sees it, with the aortic valve at 12 o'clock and the LA appendage on the left[105](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-105).

### After the procedure

After the procedure TTE is the main study[80](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-80); at 6 months it shows the LV's remodeling[85](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-85). A vena contracta of 0.7 cm or more in any jet is specific for severe residual MR; with several jets, 3D TEE adds the jets' vena contracta areas[80](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-80) ( [chapter 15](https://intro2echo.com/mr)). In one series of 66 patients the iatrogenic ASD persisted in 50% at 6 months, and its persistence went with a higher mortality[106](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-106); routine closure is not recommended[97](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-97). The peak velocity across it gives the LA–RA pressure difference, 4v² ( [bonus chapter B8](https://intro2echo.com/shunts)). In EXPAND, a clip attached to one leaflet only in 1.8% of patients, most often after the posterior leaflet came free[107](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-107).

### Key points

1. TEE guides each step: the bicaval, short-axis and four-chamber views for the puncture, and the commissural and long-axis views with 3D for alignment and grasping[97](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-97).
2. The puncture lies high and posterior, about 4.5–5.0 cm above the point where the clip will grasp[97](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-97).
3. The arms lie square to the line of coaptation, and both leaflets drape over them and stop moving[97](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-97).
4. Residual MR is graded without PISA, and a mean gradient under 5 mmHg is preferred[80](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-80), [97](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-97).

20

## Tricuspid transcatheter edge-to-edge repair

T-TEER, step by step: what TEE shows at each step

Tricuspid transcatheter edge-to-edge repair (T-TEER) joins the septal leaflet to the anterior or the posterior leaflet with a clip, brought from the femoral vein up the inferior vena cava into the right atrium. TEE guides each step of the procedure, with fluoroscopy, and TTE shows the result.

Step Before: TTE

Before: TTEGuide in the RASteeringAlignmentGraspingAssessmentReleaseAfter: TTE

Step1 of 8

Tricuspid regurgitationmassive

Vena contracta1.6cm

**Massive secondary TR.** The RV and the annulus are dilated, and the leaflets no longer meet: a central gap lets a wide jet into the RA. Vena contracta 1.6 cm, PISA EROA 0.65 cm², in the massive grade of the five-grade scale[119](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-119); the hepatic veins show systolic flow reversal[29](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-29).

### The device

A steerable guide catheter enters the femoral vein, usually the right one, and runs up the inferior vena cava to the right atrium[108](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-108), [109](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-109). Its shaft is 25 Fr[110](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-110). The delivery system goes through it and carries the implant: two rigid arms, with a flexible gripper over each that drops on its own[109](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-109). The arms are 9 or 12 mm long, and 4 or 6 mm wide[109](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-109). The FDA approved a tricuspid TEER system on April 1, 2024, to improve quality of life and functional status in symptomatic severe TR despite optimal medical therapy, at intermediate or greater surgical risk, when a heart team judges the repair appropriate and expects TR of moderate or less[111](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-111).

### Who is treated

TRILUMINATE Pivotal randomly assigned patients with symptomatic severe TR to TEER with medical therapy or to medical therapy alone[112](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-112). In the first 350 patients, the hierarchical primary end point (death or tricuspid surgery, then heart failure hospitalization, then a gain of at least 15 points in the KCCQ score at 1 year) favored TEER, with a win ratio of 1.48[112](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-112). At 30 days, TR was moderate or less in 87.0% and 4.8%; at 1 year, the KCCQ score rose by 12.3 and 0.6 points[112](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-112). Death or surgery and heart failure hospitalization did not appear to differ at 1 year; quality of life carried the result[112](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-112), [113](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-113). In all 572 randomized patients, heart failure hospitalizations through 2 years were fewer with TEER (0.19 against 0.26 per patient-year), mortality was 17.9% and 17.1%, and 61.5% of the controls crossed over to a tricuspid intervention[114](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-114). In Tri.Fr (300 patients), a composite clinical score improved at 1 year in 74.1% with TEER and 40.6% with medical therapy alone[115](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-115).

The 2025 ESC/EACTS guidelines give transcatheter treatment a class IIa recommendation (level A) to improve quality of life and RV remodeling in high-risk patients with symptomatic severe TR despite optimal medical therapy, without severe RV dysfunction or pre-capillary pulmonary hypertension[84](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-84).

### The anatomy for TEER

TRILUMINATE excluded a coaptation defect over 2 cm, calcium in the grasping area, and leads that would prevent placement of the clip[113](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-113), and a valve area of 1.0 cm² or less or a mean gradient of 5 mmHg or more[110](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-110). A small gap and a central or anteroseptal jet predicted success in an early series[116](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-116); in bRIGHT, the 30-day reduction of TR was similar for gaps under 7 mm, 7–10 mm and over 10 mm[117](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-117). In selected patients with a transvalvular pacemaker or defibrillator lead, TR was moderate or less at 30 days as often as without one (88% and 87%)[118](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-118).

### The steps on TEE

1. **Guide in the RA.** The guide comes up the IVC. The bicaval view at about 90°, with biplane or live 3D, tracks it clear of the septum, the Eustachian valve, a Chiari network and leads[109](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-109).
2. **Steering.** The delivery system flexes down toward the annulus; the imager follows it from the bicaval to the four-chamber view, then to the RV inflow view and its biplane pair[109](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-109), [108](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-108).
3. **Alignment.** In the RA the arms open, and the clip turns until they lie square to the target line of coaptation[108](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-108): most accurately on the 3D en face view, or on the transgastric short axis[109](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-109). Without a focal defect, the anteroseptal line, usually the longest, comes first[109](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-109). The septal leaflet is grasped with the anterior or the posterior leaflet: a grasp of the anterior and posterior leaflets alone has not been shown to reduce TR[108](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-108). In the RA, each gripper is lowered and raised in turn to check its leaflet[109](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-109), [108](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-108).
4. **Grasping.** The clip is closed before it goes below the valve, to keep the leaflets clear of the grippers[108](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-108). There the arms open to 120–150°, the delivery catheter comes back, and the grippers drop onto the leaflets, slowly and in diastole[108](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-108). 2D views at 0–20° or about 140–160°, the biplane pair from the RV inflow-outflow view with its cursor through the device, or 3D multiplanar reconstruction guide the grasp[109](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-109). The systolic bounce of the grippers may not show on the low-pressure right side[109](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-109); when it shows, it confirms the capture[108](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-108). A tissue bridge on the transgastric short axis or in 3D confirms the insertion; the residual leaflet length, used on the mitral valve, is less reliable here, as the leaflets stretch[108](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-108).
5. **Assessment.** Before release, TEE checks the insertion again, the reduction of TR and the gradient[108](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-108). The 3D vena contracta area grades the residual TR best, as PISA tends to overestimate it with a clip in place[109](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-109). TVARC asks for a mean gradient under 5 mmHg[119](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-119). In the TriValve registry (top quartile 4.7 mmHg), a higher discharge gradient was not associated with death or heart failure hospitalization at 1 year[120](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-120).
6. **Release.** The clip is released, the delivery catheter comes out of it, and the system is withdrawn[108](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-108). In TRILUMINATE, a mean of 2.2 clips was implanted, two or three in about 85% of the patients[113](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-113); the figure shows the first.

The guidelines show the tricuspid valve en face with the septal leaflet at 6 o'clock, from either side[105](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-105), as the figure does. Many interventional imagers skip that turn: the septum is then on the right and the aorta near 5 o'clock[86](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-86).

### After the procedure

In TRILUMINATE, 98.3% of the patients treated were free of major adverse events at 30 days: cardiovascular death, new renal failure, endocarditis needing surgery, or surgery for a device problem[112](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-112), [110](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-110). Single-leaflet attachment was the main reason for a missed device success in the randomized device arm (11 of 18 patients)[113](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-113), and occurred in 5 of 72 (7%) in the first single-arm study[121](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-121). The mean gradient rose by 1.15 mmHg at 12 months with the clip and by 0.07 mmHg without it[113](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-113). At 2 years, 84% of the device group of all 572 patients had TR of moderate or less[114](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-114). In the bRIGHT registry (511 patients), TR was moderate or less in 77% at 30 days and 81% at 1 year[122](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-122), [123](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-123).

### Key points

1. The clip joins the septal leaflet to the anterior or the posterior leaflet, and the anteroseptal line usually comes first[108](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-108), [109](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-109).
2. Residual TR is graded by 3D vena contracta area rather than PISA, and a mean gradient under 5 mmHg is the target[109](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-109), [119](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-119).
3. In TRILUMINATE, the repair reduced TR and quality of life improved; death or tricuspid surgery and heart failure hospitalization did not appear to differ at 1 year[112](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-112).

21

## Diseases of the aorta

Root size, aneurysm, dissection and coarctation

Echocardiography measures the aortic root and the ascending aorta against normal values and surgical thresholds. It also shows the flap of a dissection and its complications, and the Doppler signs of coarctation.

Severity aneurysm, 4.7 cm

**Aneurysm of the ascending aorta, 4.7 cm.** At end-diastole, L-L, the sinuses measure 4.1 cm, the STJ 4.0 cm and the ascending aorta 4.7 cm. The annulus measures 2.5 cm, I-I at midsystole. For a man aged 50–59 years with a BSA of 2.0 m², the sinus Z score is 2.5; a Z score of 2 or more is dilated[124](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-124). An aneurysm starts at 4.5 cm. At surgery on a tricuspid aortic valve, replacement of an ascending aorta of 4.5 cm or more is reasonable with experienced surgeons (2a)[125](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-125).

| Tricuspid aortic valve | – | Replacement reasonable at aortic valve surgery, experienced team (2a) | Surgery reasonable, experienced team (2a) | Surgery indicated (1) |
| Bicuspid aortic valve | Lifelong surveillance imaging (1) | Replacement reasonable at aortic valve surgery, experienced team (2a) | Surgery reasonable with a risk factor (2a); may be reasonable without one, at low surgical risk (2b) | Surgery indicated (1) |

The largest diameter of the root or the ascending aorta (ACC/AHA 2022), in an asymptomatic patient

|
|

### Measuring the root and the ascending aorta

The root and proximal ascending aorta are imaged best in the left parasternal long-axis view[124](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-124). The annulus is measured at midsystole, inner edge to inner edge (I-I). The sinuses of Valsalva at their largest diameter, the sinotubular junction (STJ) and the proximal ascending aorta are measured at end-diastole, leading edge to leading edge (L-L), perpendicular to the aortic long axis[14](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-14). CT and MRI measure I-I[125](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-125), and the echocardiographic L-L diameter is on average 2 mm larger than the I-I diameter on CT[124](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-124).

Lang 2015 gives mean diameters in men: the annulus 2.6 ± 0.3 cm, the sinuses 3.4 ± 0.3 cm, the STJ 2.9 ± 0.3 cm and the ascending aorta 3.0 ± 0.4 cm[14](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-14). The figure draws the normal ranges as the mean ± 2 SD and measures the ascending aorta 1.5 cm above the STJ. In Goldstein 2015, the upper limit of normal by age and BSA lies 2 SD above the predicted mean, and a Z score of 2 or more is dilated[124](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-124). For a man aged 50–59 years at a BSA of 2.0 m², the mean is 3.6 cm and the upper limit 4.0 cm[124](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-124). The figure gives his sinus Z score.

### Dilation and aneurysm

The 2022 ACC/AHA guideline calls a diameter of 4.0–4.4 cm dilated and one of 4.5 cm or more an aneurysm[125](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-125). Without symptoms, surgery on the root or the ascending aorta is indicated at 5.5 cm or more (class 1), and is reasonable at 5.0 cm or more with experienced surgeons in a Multidisciplinary Aortic Team (2a)[125](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-125). Growth of 0.3 cm/year or more in 2 consecutive years, or of 0.5 cm or more in 1 year, confirmed by tomographic imaging, also indicates surgery (class 1)[125](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-125). At surgery on a tricuspid aortic valve, replacement of an ascending aorta of 4.5 cm or more is reasonable with experienced surgeons (2a)[125](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-125).

With a bicuspid aortic valve, the same 5.5-cm threshold applies[125](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-125). At 5.0–5.4 cm, surgery is reasonable with a risk factor for dissection (2a): a family history of aortic dissection, growth of 0.3 cm/year or more, aortic coarctation, or the root phenotype. Without one, at low surgical risk, it may be reasonable (2b)[125](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-125). At aortic valve surgery, replacement of a root or an ascending aorta of 4.5 cm or more is reasonable with experienced surgeons (2a), and from 4.0 cm lifelong surveillance imaging is recommended (class 1)[125](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-125).

### Aortic dissection

The diagnostic hallmark of dissection is a mobile flap that separates the true and the false lumen. The flap moves independently of the aorta, is seen in more than one view and is clearly distinct from reverberations[124](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-124). In systole the true lumen expands, and the flap typically moves toward the false lumen[124](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-124). In the figure, the entry tear lies beyond the view.

Reverberations, especially in the ascending aorta, can mimic a flap[124](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-124). In the figure, the reverberation of the pericardium in front of the aorta lies at twice its depth ( [chapter 02](https://intro2echo.com/image)), moves with it, and runs across the lumen into the posterior wall. From the zoomed window, one space higher, it lies elsewhere, and on the M-mode it follows the pericardium at twice its excursion. A flap stays in the aorta and moves on its own.

AR occurs in about 50% of patients with type A dissection[124](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-124). Most often, the dilated root tethers the cusps, so that they do not close fully. A flap that extends into the sinuses can detach the commissures, so that the cusps prolapse in diastole. Less often, a long flap prolapses through the valve into the LVOT[124](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-124). An eccentric jet often points away from the cusp at fault[29](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-29). Along the slider, the figure shows the three mechanisms in turn. It grades the AR by its vena contracta: below 0.3 cm mild, above 0.6 cm severe[29](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-29) ( [chapter 13](https://intro2echo.com/ar)). A pericardial effusion in ascending dissection indicates a poor prognosis and suggests rupture of the false lumen into the pericardium[124](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-124).

With harmonic imaging, TTE detects about 85% of type A dissections and is less sensitive for type B[124](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-124). A negative TTE does not exclude dissection[124](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-124). In a meta-analysis, TEE had a sensitivity of 98% and a specificity of 95%[124](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-124).

### Coarctation

Coarctation most commonly lies just distal to the left subclavian artery[124](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-124). The suprasternal view with color flow often finds it, but TTE often images the arch and the proximal descending aorta poorly[124](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-124). In a significant coarctation, the CW gradient persists into diastole: the diastolic tail[124](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-124), [126](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-126). PW Doppler of the abdominal aorta then shows reduced, delayed systolic flow and forward flow through diastole[124](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-124).

The ACC/AHA requires upper-extremity hypertension or LVH and one of three findings above 20 mmHg: the arm–leg systolic pressure difference, the catheter peak-to-peak gradient or the mean Doppler gradient. With reduced LV function or significant collateral flow, a gradient above 10 mmHg counts[125](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-125). For the ESC, an arm–leg difference of 20 mmHg or more, a diastolic tail, diastolic forward flow in the abdominal aorta, or a catheter peak-to-peak gradient of 20 mmHg or more without well-developed collaterals indicates a significant coarctation[126](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-126). The ESC does not use Doppler gradients: collaterals make them unreliable and often low, and after repair or stenting they are overestimated[126](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-126). It considers stenting in a hypertensive patient with a narrowing of 50% or more relative to the aorta at the diaphragm, even with a peak-to-peak gradient below 20 mmHg (IIa)[126](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-126).

The figure compares the narrowest lumen with the abdominal aorta below the diaphragm. Its patient has upper-extremity hypertension, normal LV function and no significant collateral flow, and every criterion of both guidelines turns at the same point of the slider. The mean gradient is 4V² averaged over the systolic envelope.

### Key points

1. The sinuses, STJ and ascending aorta are measured L-L at end-diastole, the annulus I-I at midsystole[14](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-14).
2. A root or ascending aorta of 4.0–4.4 cm is dilated, and one of 4.5 cm or more an aneurysm. Surgery is indicated at 5.5 cm, with a tricuspid or a bicuspid valve[125](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-125).
3. A dissection flap moves independently of the aorta and is seen in more than one view[124](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-124). A negative TTE does not exclude dissection[124](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-124).
4. In a significant coarctation, the CW gradient persists into diastole. The ACC/AHA counts a mean Doppler gradient above 20 mmHg; the ESC uses no Doppler gradient[125](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-125), [126](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-126).

B5

## Pericardial disease

Effusion, tamponade and constriction

A pericardial effusion compresses the heart once the pressure in the sac reaches the pressure in the right heart, a condition termed tamponade. Echocardiography grades the effusion and shows the signs of tamponade. Less often, a thick, rigid pericardium fixes the volume of the heart: constrictive pericarditis.

Effusionmoderate

Grademoderate

**Moderate pericardial effusion.** The echo-free space measures 15 mm in diastole, consistent with a moderate effusion. The pressure in the pericardial sac (2 mmHg) remains below the RA pressure (5 mmHg), so the cardiac chambers fill normally; the IVC measures 1.8 cm and collapses by 50% with a sniff, and the mitral E velocity varies by 8% with respiration.

| Parameter | Mild (small) | Moderate | Severe (large) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Echo-free space at end-diastole | <10 mm | 10–20 mm | >20 mm |
| Volume, approximate (ASE) | 50–100 mL | 100–500 mL | >500 mL |

Effusion size

| Parameter | Without tamponade | Tamponade |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Right atrium | Brief or no inversion | Inversion for more than one third of the cycle |
| Right ventricle | Free wall moves outward in diastole | Free wall collapses in early diastole |
| IVC | ≤2.1 cm, collapses 50% or more | >2.1 cm, collapses less than 50% |
| Mitral E with inspiration | Falls less than 25% | Falls more than 25–30% |
| Tricuspid E with inspiration | Rises less than 40% | Rises more than 40–60% |
| Hepatic veins | Forward flow in systole and diastole | Diastolic flow falls or reverses in expiration |

Echocardiographic signs of tamponade

### Effusion size and progression to tamponade

1. **None.** The normal pericardial sac contains 10–50 mL of fluid[127](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-127). A separation of the pericardial layers seen only in systole is a trivial effusion; a separation in both systole and diastole indicates more than 50 mL of fluid[128](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-128).
2. **Mild (small).** The echo-free space between the epicardium and the parietal pericardium, measured at end-diastole, is less than 10 mm[127](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-127). The ASE guideline assigns 50–100 mL to a small effusion and describes these volumes as arbitrary[128](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-128).
3. **Moderate.** The echo-free space measures 10–20 mm[127](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-127), which corresponds to approximately 100–500 mL[128](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-128). In this model, the pressure in the sac remains below the pressure in the right heart, and at most a brief RA inversion occurs.
4. **Severe (large).** The echo-free space exceeds 20 mm[127](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-127); the ASE guideline defines an additional very large category of more than 25 mm[128](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-128). The heart can swing within the fluid, and the ESC guideline associates this swinging motion with electrical alternans on the ECG[127](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-127).
5. **Tamponade.** The pressure in the sac reaches the pressure in the right heart. A cardiac chamber collapses during its relaxation phase, when the pressure within it falls below the pressure in the sac[128](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-128).

### Echocardiographic signs of tamponade

- **Right atrium.** The RA wall inverts near the peak of the R wave and remains inverted until atrial filling during ventricular systole raises the atrial pressure[128](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-128). RA collapse for more than one third of the cardiac cycle is nearly 100% sensitive and specific for tamponade[128](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-128). In the original study, an RA collapse time of more than 0.34 of the cardiac cycle had a sensitivity of 94% and a specificity of 100%[129](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-129).
- **Right ventricle.** The RV free wall collapses in early diastole, after the T wave[128](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-128). RV diastolic collapse occurs when the cardiac output has decreased by about 20% but the systemic blood pressure has not fallen[128](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-128).
- **Left atrium.** LA collapse also occurs in about one quarter of patients with tamponade and is a highly specific sign[130](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-130).
- **IVC.** The IVC is wider than 2.1 cm and collapses by less than 50% with inspiration. This finding is present in more than 90% of patients with tamponade, but many other cardiac diseases also cause it[128](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-128). The ESC 2025 guideline uses an IVC diameter cut-off of 20 mm, which has a sensitivity of 97% and a specificity of 40%[131](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-131). [Chapter 07](https://intro2echo.com/pressure) shows the measurement of the IVC and the RA pressure that it gives.
- **Respiratory variation of inflow.** With inspiration, the mitral E velocity falls by more than 25–30% and the tricuspid E velocity increases by more than 40–60%[131](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-131). The RV enlarges and the LV becomes smaller during inspiration, and the reverse occurs during expiration[128](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-128).
- **Hepatic veins.** Diastolic forward flow in the hepatic veins decreases or reverses on the first beat of expiration[128](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-128). When the IVC is difficult to assess, dilated hepatic veins confirm a raised systemic venous pressure[128](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-128). [Chapter 07](https://intro2echo.com/pressure) shows the normal hepatic vein waves.

### Pitfalls

- Effusion size does not define tamponade; the rate of fluid accumulation is more important than the volume[127](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-127). Rapid accumulation, as in hemopericardium, can cause tamponade at 200–300 mL, whereas a slowly accumulating effusion can reach 1–2 L before tamponade develops[131](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-131).
- Tamponade is a clinical diagnosis based on the history, symptoms and signs and confirmed by echocardiography[131](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-131). Pulsus paradoxus is an inspiratory fall in systolic blood pressure of more than 10 mmHg[127](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-127).
- A brief RA inversion also occurs in the absence of tamponade, so the duration of the inversion should be measured. An M-mode recording through the RA wall displays the timing most clearly[128](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-128).
- The absence of chamber collapse has a negative predictive value of more than 90% for tamponade. RV hypertrophy, severe pulmonary hypertension or severe LV dysfunction can delay or prevent chamber collapse[128](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-128).
- Pericardial fluid is distinguished from pleural fluid in the parasternal long-axis view, in which pericardial fluid lies between the descending aorta and the heart and pleural fluid extends posterior to the descending aorta[128](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-128).
- Respiratory variation of inflow is not a diagnostic criterion on its own. During positive-pressure ventilation, the variation is minimal, even in tamponade[128](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-128).
- In effusive-constrictive pericarditis, fluid lies between a thickened visceral and parietal pericardium[128](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-128), and after pericardiocentesis the RA pressure does not fall by 50% or to below 10 mmHg[131](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-131).

### Constrictive pericarditis

In constrictive pericarditis a thickened, scarred and often calcified pericardium fixes the total volume of the heart, so the diastolic pressures of the chambers rise and equalize[128](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-128). The inspiratory fall in intrathoracic pressure reaches the pulmonary veins but not the heart chambers, so LV filling falls with inspiration, RV filling rises and the septum shifts toward the LV. Expiration reverses both, and blood flows back into the hepatic veins[128](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-128).

- **Septum.** The septum shifts toward the LV with inspiration and moves abruptly toward the LV in early diastole, the septal bounce[128](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-128). Without a plethoric IVC and a respiratory septal shift, constriction is unlikely[132](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-132).
- **Inflow.** With inspiration the mitral E velocity falls by more than 25% and the tricuspid E velocity rises by more than 40%[128](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-128), [131](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-131).
- **Annulus and hepatic veins.** The medial mitral e′ is normal or increased[128](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-128). In expiration the hepatic vein flow reverses at end-diastole[128](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-128); the reversal ratio is the end-diastolic reversal velocity ÷ the diastolic forward velocity[17](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-17), [132](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-132).
- **Pericardium.** The normal pericardium is 1–2 mm thick on CT. A thickness above 4 mm on CT or CMR supports constriction, and TTE is unreliable for measuring it[128](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-128). Up to 20% of patients with constriction have a pericardium of normal thickness[131](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-131).

In the Mayo Clinic series of surgically confirmed constriction, a respiratory septal shift with a medial e′ of 9 cm/s or more, or with a hepatic vein expiratory diastolic reversal ratio of 0.79 or more, had a sensitivity of 87% and a specificity of 91%[133](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-133). The guidelines give the septal shift no size; this page calls it present from 3 mm.

### Constriction or restrictive cardiomyopathy

In restrictive cardiomyopathy, for example cardiac amyloidosis ( [bonus chapter B4](https://intro2echo.com/amyloid)), the stiff myocardium, not the pericardium, limits filling. The septal e′ is usually 5 cm/s or less[17](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-17), and the marked respiratory changes in mitral and tricuspid E do not occur[134](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-134).

### Key points

1. Effusion size is graded by the end-diastolic echo-free space: mild <10 mm, moderate 10–20 mm and severe (large) >20 mm. The rate of fluid accumulation, rather than the size of the effusion, determines how well the effusion is tolerated.
2. RA collapse for more than one third of the cardiac cycle and RV free wall collapse in early diastole indicate tamponade physiology. Tamponade is a clinical diagnosis.
3. A dilated IVC that collapses little is present in more than 90% of patients with tamponade, but many other cardiac diseases also cause it.
4. In constriction, inspiration shifts the septum toward the LV and lowers mitral E. The Mayo Clinic criteria pair the septal shift with a medial e′ of 9 cm/s or more or a hepatic vein reversal ratio of 0.79 or more.

B4

## Cardiac amyloidosis

Echocardiographic signs

In cardiac amyloidosis, amyloid fibrils accumulate between the myocardial cells. The LV walls thicken around a small cavity, LV filling becomes restrictive, and the base loses longitudinal shortening before the apex. These echocardiographic findings raise the suspicion of cardiac amyloidosis.

The Doppler findings indicate grade 2 diastolic dysfunction, with an E/A ratio of 1.21, a deceleration time of 187 ms and an average E/e′ of 13.3[17](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-17). GLS is −14.0% and the relative apical LS is 0.82[138](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-138).

The criteria raise the suspicion of cardiac amyloidosis. In the ESC diagnostic pathways, they are used together with other tests[135](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-135).

### Structural findings

- **LV wall thickness.** The LV wall thickness is 12 mm or more in the absence of another cause, such as aortic valve disease or hypertension[137](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-137). Used alone, this cut-off has high sensitivity and low specificity[137](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-137).
- **LV cavity.** The LV cavity is small and the relative wall thickness is high. In a study of 1,187 patients, concentric remodeling was among the best single diagnostic signs[136](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-136).
- **Atria and interatrial septum.** Both atria enlarge; an LA volume index of more than 34 mL/m² is abnormal[14](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-14). An interatrial septum thicker than 5 mm is characteristic, and the valve leaflets thicken[137](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-137).
- **Right ventricle.** The RV free wall is measured at end-diastole in the parasternal long-axis or subcostal view[137](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-137). A free wall thickness of more than 5 mm indicates RV hypertrophy[33](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-33). RV hypertrophy together with a thick LV wall suggests an infiltrative disease[137](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-137).
- **Pericardium.** A pericardial effusion is present in more than half of patients but is not specific[137](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-137).
- **Myocardial texture.** A granular, “sparkling” or “speckled” myocardium was long described as a sign of amyloidosis. The BSE advises against the term, because other diseases produce the same appearance and harmonic imaging ( [chapter 02](https://intro2echo.com/image)) confounds it[137](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-137).

### Doppler findings

- Diastolic dysfunction progresses from slowed relaxation with a normal filling pressure (grade 1) to restrictive filling with an E/A ratio of 2 or more (grade 3)[17](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-17) ( [chapter 09](https://intro2echo.com/fill)).
- A clear restrictive filling pattern seldom appears before the late stages of the disease[137](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-137). In these stages, the deceleration time falls below 150 ms and the septal and lateral e′ velocities fall to 3–4 cm/s[17](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-17).
- The “5-5-5” sign, in which the septal s′, e′ and a′ velocities are all below 5 cm/s, is one of the three findings of the ESC echocardiographic criteria[135](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-135).
- A small A wave suggests reduced atrial function and a higher risk of atrial thrombus[137](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-137).

### Longitudinal strain

- Longitudinal strain is reduced most at the base. On the bull’s-eye plot ( [chapter 10](https://intro2echo.com/segments)), the apical segments remain red while the basal segments become pale, a pattern termed relative apical sparing[138](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-138).
- Relative apical LS is a ratio of regional strain values: the mean apical strain divided by the sum of the mean basal and mean mid-ventricular strain. In the original study, a value of 1.0 or more distinguished amyloidosis from hypertrophic cardiomyopathy and aortic stenosis with a sensitivity of 93% and a specificity of 82%[138](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-138).
- A multicenter study found apical sparing in 32% of patients in whom amyloidosis was suspected and then excluded, and in 6% of healthy individuals[139](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-139).
- The base-to-apex strain gradient corresponds to the total amyloid mass in each region, which is largest at the base[140](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-140).
- An EF ÷ \|GLS\| ratio of more than 4.1 distinguished amyloidosis from other causes of increased LV wall thickness with a sensitivity of 89.7% and a specificity of 91.7%[141](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-141). A normal EF can mask impaired long-axis function.
- GLS is normal when more negative than −18% and abnormal when less negative than −16%[39](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-39) ( [chapter 08](https://intro2echo.com/eject)). The ESC criteria use a stricter cut-off: an absolute GLS below 15%[135](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-135).

### ESC 2021 echocardiographic criteria

The criteria require an unexplained LV wall thickness of 12 mm or more together with either two of the three findings or a score of 8 or more[135](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-135); the diagram above applies them to the values on screen. The score assigns points for a thick wall around a small cavity, a high E/e′, a low TAPSE ( [chapter 08](https://intro2echo.com/eject)), a low GLS and relative sparing of the apical septum[136](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-136). The criteria raise the suspicion of cardiac amyloidosis. In the ESC diagnostic pathways, they are used together with other tests[135](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-135).

### Measurement

- Wall thickness is measured at end-diastole, perpendicular to the LV long axis, at the level of the mitral leaflet tips[14](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-14).
- In the score, relative wall thickness is calculated as (IVS + PW) ÷ LVIDd[135](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-135); the ASE guideline defines it as 2 × PW ÷ LVIDd, with a cut-off of 0.42[14](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-14).

### Key points

1. An LV wall thickness of 12 mm or more without another cause, a small LV cavity and enlargement of both atria suggest cardiac amyloidosis.
2. Restrictive filling, septal s′, e′ and a′ velocities below 5 cm/s and relative apical sparing of longitudinal strain support the diagnosis.
3. The echocardiographic criteria raise the suspicion of cardiac amyloidosis but do not establish the diagnosis alone.

B3

## Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy

Septal hypertrophy, SAM and outflow obstruction

In hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (HCM), the LV wall is thick without another cause, most often at the basal anterior septum. In most patients, systolic anterior motion of the mitral valve obstructs the LV outflow tract, at rest or only with provocation.

Severity obstruction at rest

wall <13 mmwall 13–14 mmwall ≥15 mmlatent obstructionobstruction at rest

Maneuver
