Frame

CineEnd-diastoleSAM

Maximal wall20mm

Septum ÷ posterior wall2.22

LVOT gradient, rest31mmHg · 2.8 m/s

SAMmoderatecontact 19%

MRmoderateVC 0.38 cm

LVEF70%

**Obstructive hypertrophic cardiomyopathy.** The anterior septum measures 20 mm at end-diastole and the posterior wall 9 mm, a ratio of 2.22, above the 1.3 of asymmetric septal hypertrophy in a normotensive patient[143](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-143). The peak LVOT velocity is 2.8 m/s at rest, a gradient of 31 mmHg, and 3.3 m/s with Valsalva (44 mmHg): obstruction at rest (30 mmHg or more)[142](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-142). At rest, the anterior mitral leaflet meets the septum for 19% of systole (moderate SAM)[143](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-143). The leaflets part, with a posteriorly directed MR jet; its vena contracta is 0.38 cm (moderate)[29](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-29). The MR jet reaches 5.7 m/s, faster than the LVOT jet[143](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-143). The LVEF is 70%, the LV end-diastolic diameter 39 mm and the LA volume index 34 mL/m² (normal)[14](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-14). The longer the contact, the higher the gradient[144](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-144).

| Parameter | Non-obstructive | Latent | At rest |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Peak LVOT gradient at rest | <30 mmHg | <30 mmHg | ≥30 mmHg |
| With provocation (Valsalva, standing, exercise) | <30 mmHg | ≥30 mmHg | — |

LVOT obstruction (ASE 2022, AHA/ACC 2024)

| Parameter | Mild | Moderate | Severe |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| SAM on M-mode | SAM–septal distance >10 mm | ≤10 mm, or contact <30% of systole | Contact ≥30% of systole |

Severity of SAM (ASE 2022)

### Definition and measurement

In an adult, HCM is defined by a maximal end-diastolic wall thickness of 15 mm or more anywhere in the LV, without another cause of hypertrophy[142](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-142). A wall of 13–14 mm can be diagnostic in a family member of a patient with HCM or with a positive genetic test[142](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-142). The normal septum and posterior wall measure 6–9 mm in women and 6–10 mm in men[14](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-14).

The most common pattern is focal asymmetric hypertrophy of the basal anterior septum, defined as a septal to posterior wall thickness ratio above 1.3 in a normotensive patient. The variants are a sigmoid septum, reversed septal curvature, and concentric, mid-wall and apical hypertrophy[143](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-143). RV trabeculations, the moderator band and the crista supraventricularis are excluded from the septum. A long-axis view can cut the wall tangentially and overestimate it, so the short- and long-axis views are compared[143](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-143).

### SAM and dynamic obstruction

Obstruction of the LV outflow tract (LVOT) is caused mainly by systolic anterior motion (SAM) of the mitral valve[142](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-142). Drag forces of the flow push the anterior leaflet toward the septum, and SAM can begin at a normal LVOT velocity[143](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-143). Most patients with obstruction have abnormal mitral leaflets or papillary muscles; elongated leaflets predispose to SAM[143](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-143). SAM is not specific to HCM: a low afterload, a low preload or a high inotropic state can also cause it[143](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-143).

On M-mode through the mitral valve, SAM is mild when the leaflet stays more than 10 mm from the septum. It is moderate at 10 mm or less, or with contact for less than 30% of systole, and severe with contact for 30% of systole or more[143](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-143). With simultaneous catheterization, SAM without septal contact went with no gradient or a gradient below 10 mmHg, and contact always went with a gradient above 10 mmHg. The earlier the contact began, the longer it lasted and the higher the gradient was: 25 × (the duration of contact ÷ the time from the onset of SAM to contact) + 25 mmHg[144](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-144).

### LVOT gradient and provocation

- The peak LVOT velocity is recorded with CW Doppler, and the gradient is 4 × velocity²[143](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-143). Obstruction is present at a peak gradient of 30 mmHg or more[142](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-142), [145](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-145).
- The obstruction is dynamic: it varies with preload, afterload and contractility[142](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-142). When the resting peak gradient is below 50 mmHg, the echocardiogram is repeated with provocation: standing, the strain phase of the Valsalva maneuver or exercise[142](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-142). The Valsalva effort varies, and its gradient is generally lower than an exercise gradient[143](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-143).
- About 30–35% of symptomatic patients have obstruction at rest, and another 30–35% have latent obstruction, present only with provocation[143](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-143).
- Exercise echocardiography ( [bonus chapter B1](https://intro2echo.com/stress)) is recommended for symptomatic patients without a resting or provoked gradient of 50 mmHg or more[142](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-142), [145](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-145). A resting or provoked gradient of 50 mmHg or more is the threshold for advanced drug or invasive therapy when symptoms persist despite standard therapy[142](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-142).

### The LVOT jet and the MR jet

- The LVOT velocity rises slowly in early systole, then rises abruptly and peaks in mid-to-late systole, a dagger-shaped envelope[143](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-143). In aortic stenosis or a discrete subaortic membrane, the envelope peaks earlier, and the absence of SAM suggests a fixed obstruction[143](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-143) ( [chapter 12](https://intro2echo.com/as)).
- MR usually accompanies SAM, and its jet lies close to the LVOT. The CW beam is swept from the LVOT into the MR jet, and each signal is recorded and labeled[143](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-143). Compared with the LVOT jet, the MR jet usually peaks earlier and can last longer, its envelope is rounder, and its velocity is always higher. A velocity above 5.5 m/s is taken as LVOT flow only with caution[143](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-143).
- The MR jet is eccentric and directed posteriorly and laterally[143](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-143), and it is typically mid-to-late systolic[142](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-142). A central or anterior jet prompts a search for an intrinsic abnormality of the mitral valve, although SAM alone can also produce such a jet[143](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-143). The MR varies with the obstruction, so both are assessed at rest and with provocation[142](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-142). The MR is graded as in [chapter 15](https://intro2echo.com/mr): a vena contracta below 0.3 cm is mild and one of 0.7 cm or more is severe[29](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-29).

### LV function, the LA and risk

- The LVEF is normal to hyperdynamic in most patients. An LVEF below 50% defines LV dysfunction[143](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-143).
- Four measurements estimate the LA pressure in HCM: an average E/e′ above 14, an LA volume index above 34 mL/m², an Ar−A duration of 30 ms or more and a peak TR velocity above 2.8 m/s[17](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-17) ( [chapter 09](https://intro2echo.com/fill)).
- Three of the major risk factors for sudden death are echocardiographic: a maximal wall thickness of 30 mm or more in any segment, an LV apical aneurysm and an LVEF below 50%[142](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-142).

### Differential diagnosis

Asymmetric hypertrophy and dynamic obstruction are not pathognomonic of HCM[143](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-143).

- **Athlete’s heart.** The wall is typically thinner than 15 mm, the four chambers dilate in balance, and the diastolic function is normal or supranormal[143](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-143). A dilated LV with uniform thickening, a normal mitral valve and no obstruction at rest or with exercise favor physiologic hypertrophy[143](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-143).
- **Hypertension.** Hypertensive heart disease most commonly gives concentric hypertrophy or remodeling, and the ratio of 1.3 applies to a normotensive patient[143](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-143).
- **Cardiac amyloidosis.** The LV wall thickens concentrically, sometimes more at the septum. Global longitudinal strain falls with relative apical sparing, and the QRS voltage is low for the wall thickness[143](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-143) ( [bonus chapter B4](https://intro2echo.com/amyloid)).

### The figure

The contact timing sets the gradient by the regression of Pollick[144](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-144). The Valsalva switch shows the strain phase, with earlier contact and a higher gradient. The two CW strips share one velocity scale. The MR strip records both jets, as a beam between them does: the MR envelope and, inside it, the brighter LVOT envelope. The patient and the numbers are illustrative.

### Key points

1. HCM is a maximal end-diastolic LV wall thickness of 15 mm or more without another cause; 13–14 mm can be diagnostic in a family member or with a positive genetic test.
2. SAM causes dynamic LVOT obstruction; contact with the septum for 30% of systole or more is severe SAM.
3. Obstruction is a peak LVOT gradient of 30 mmHg or more, at rest or with provocation. A resting gradient below 50 mmHg calls for provocation.
4. On CW Doppler, the LVOT jet is dagger-shaped and peaks late. The MR jet peaks earlier, is rounder and is always faster.

B2

## Strain

Global longitudinal strain by speckle tracking, its patterns, and cardio-oncology

Longitudinal strain is the shortening of the myocardium from end-diastole to end-systole, as a percentage of its length at end-diastole. Global longitudinal strain (GLS) can fall while the LVEF is still normal. During cancer therapy, its relative fall from baseline signals cardiac dysfunction. Relative apical sparing characterizes cardiac amyloidosis.

**Abnormal GLS with a normal LVEF.** The midline of the four-chamber view measures 21.19 cm at end-diastole and 17.95 cm at end-systole, a global strain of −15.3% for that view[15](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-15). The two-chamber and three-chamber views give −15.7% and −13.6%, and GLS, the mean of the three, is −14.9%: abnormal, less negative than −16%[39](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-39). The 18 segments read −18% to −9%. The LVEF by the biplane method is 59%, normal for a woman (54–74%)[14](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-14). GLS can fall while the LVEF stays normal, as it does early in cancer therapy[39](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-39).

LVEF by the biplane method (ASE/EACVI 2015)

### Strain and speckle tracking

Strain is the change in length of the myocardium as a fraction of its length at a reference time, usually end-diastole: S = (L − L0) ÷ L0[15](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-15). Shortening gives a negative strain, so GLS becomes less negative as LV function deteriorates[15](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-15). GLS is the change in myocardial length between end-diastole and end-systole, measured in the four-, two- and three-chamber views and averaged[14](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-14). It uses the length of the whole myocardial line[15](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-15).

Speckle tracking follows the speckle pattern of the image from frame to frame, and assumes that it moves with the tissue[15](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-15). The region of interest is drawn at end-diastole; end-systole is aortic valve closure, and the end-systolic strain is the default value to report[15](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-15). GLS should not be calculated when the tracking is suboptimal in more than two segments of one view[14](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-14).

In the figure, the region of interest is the myocardial midline of each apical view, from base to base over the apex, each wall divided at end-diastole into three equal segments. The bull's-eye has 18 segments, six from each view; the 17-segment model of [chapter 10](https://intro2echo.com/segments) has four apical segments and the apex.

### Normal values

A peak GLS of about −20% can be expected in a healthy person[14](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-14). The 2025 ASE/EACVI consensus calls GLS normal when it is more negative than −18%, borderline from −16% to −18%, and abnormal when it is less negative than −16%[39](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-39). The values differ between vendors, by up to 3.7 strain units[146](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-146), so serial studies should use the same machine and software[146](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-146), [147](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-147).

The figure measures the LVEF by the biplane method of disks ( [chapter 08](https://intro2echo.com/eject)). The patient is a woman, so her LVEF is graded by the ranges for women: normal 54–74%, mildly abnormal 41–53%, moderately abnormal 30–40% and severely abnormal below 30%[14](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-14). The patient and the numbers are illustrative.

### Patterns of strain

When the LV mass is increased, the distribution of abnormal strain may give clues to the cause: impaired septal strain in hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, mostly basal and mid impairment in hypertensive heart disease, and prominent apical sparing in cardiac amyloidosis[39](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-39).

In the Global pattern, every segment loses function together. The long-axis shortening falls first, so GLS becomes abnormal while the LVEF is still normal; then the LVEF falls.

In the Regional pattern, the seven segments of the LAD territory lose function first, to akinesis; at the end of the slider, the other segments lose function too. The territories and wall motion are in [chapter 10](https://intro2echo.com/segments), and ischemia under stress in [bonus chapter B1](https://intro2echo.com/stress). Regional strain values vary too much between repeated tests and between vendors to be used clinically[39](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-39).

In the Apical sparing pattern, the basal and mid segments lose their longitudinal shortening while the apical segments keep theirs, and GLS falls. This pattern, relative apical sparing, characterizes cardiac amyloidosis[138](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-138). Late in the slider, the basal and mid segments also lose their motion across the long axis, and the LVEF falls. Relative apical LS is the mean apical strain divided by the sum of the mean basal and mean mid strain, without the signs. In the original study, a value of 1.0 or more distinguished cardiac amyloidosis from hypertrophic cardiomyopathy and aortic stenosis[138](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-138). Apical sparing also occurs without amyloidosis[139](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-139); the diagnosis of cardiac amyloidosis is in [bonus chapter B4](https://intro2echo.com/amyloid).

### Cardio-oncology

GLS falls early during treatment with anthracyclines, trastuzumab or radiotherapy, even when the LVEF is unchanged, and the early fall predicts a later fall in the LVEF[39](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-39). The 2022 ESC guideline supports the definitions of cancer therapy-related cardiac dysfunction (CTRCD) of the International Cardio-Oncology Society[147](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-147), [148](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-148). Symptomatic CTRCD is heart failure; asymptomatic CTRCD is graded by the LVEF, GLS and the cardiac biomarkers[147](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-147):

- **Mild.** An LVEF of 50% or more with a new relative decline in GLS of more than 15% from baseline and/or a new rise in cardiac biomarkers.
- **Moderate.** A new fall of the LVEF by 10 percentage points or more, to 40–49%; or a new fall by less than 10 points, to 40–49%, with either a new relative decline in GLS of more than 15% or a new rise in cardiac biomarkers.
- **Severe.** A new fall of the LVEF to below 40%[147](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-147).

A new rise in cardiac biomarkers is a troponin I or T above the 99th percentile, a BNP of 35 pg/mL or more, an NT-proBNP of 125 pg/mL or more, or a new significant rise from baseline beyond the variation of the assay[147](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-147). The guideline's cut-off of 15% improves specificity[147](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-147). In the 2014 ASE/EACVI consensus, a relative fall of less than 8% from baseline appears not to be meaningful, and a fall of more than 15% is very likely abnormal; an abnormal GLS should be confirmed by a repeat study 2 to 3 weeks later[149](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-149). The 2025 strain consensus considers a relative change of 10% to 15% on serial studies likely to be significant[39](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-39).

In the ESC guideline, echocardiography is the first-line modality for cardiac function in patients with cancer; 3D echocardiography is the preferred method for the LVEF, and GLS is recommended in every patient who has echocardiography, if available, at baseline and serially[147](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-147).

In the SUCCOUR trial, patients treated with anthracyclines were randomized to start cardioprotective therapy on a relative fall in GLS of 12% or more, or on a fall in the LVEF of more than 10 percentage points[150](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-150). The change in the LVEF, the primary outcome, did not differ between the arms at 1 year[150](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-150) or at 3 years[151](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-151); at 1 year, fewer patients in the GLS-guided arm met the trial's criteria for CTRCD[150](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-150).

### Key points

1. Longitudinal strain is (L − L0) ÷ L0 from end-diastole, negative for shortening[15](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-15). GLS can fall while the LVEF is still normal[39](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-39).
2. GLS is abnormal when less negative than −16%[39](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-39). Vendors differ, so serial studies use the same machine and software[146](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-146).
3. A relative apical LS of 1.0 or more distinguished cardiac amyloidosis from hypertrophic cardiomyopathy and aortic stenosis[138](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-138); relative apical sparing also occurs without amyloidosis[139](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-139).
4. During cancer therapy, a new relative decline in GLS of more than 15% with an LVEF of 50% or more is mild asymptomatic CTRCD; a new fall of the LVEF to below 40% is severe[147](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-147).

B1

## Stress echocardiography

Dobutamine stress, ischemia and viability

Stress echocardiography compares the wall motion at rest with the wall motion during stress. A segment that is normal at rest and becomes abnormal with stress indicates ischemia in the territory of its coronary artery.

Case

NormalLADRCALCXTwo vesselsScarViable

Peak stage40 µg/kg/min and atropine 0.75 mg

Peak heart rate138/min · 86%

Peak blood pressure148/66mmHg

WMSI, rest → peak1.00 → 1.00

LVEF, rest → peak–

ResultNormal

**Normal response.** Every segment is normal at rest and becomes hyperkinetic with dobutamine, and the LV cavity becomes smaller[152](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-152). The heart rate reaches 138/min with 40 µg/kg/min and atropine 0.75 mg, 86% of the age-predicted maximum of 160/min (target 136/min), so the stress is adequate[152](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-152).

### Principle

Stress increases the oxygen demand of the myocardium. Beyond a flow-limiting coronary stenosis, the subendocardial blood flow falls with stress, and the wall of that territory thickens less and moves inward less[152](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-152). In the ischemic cascade, myocardial contractility decreases before the ST segment changes and before angina occurs[153](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-153), and perfusion abnormalities occur before wall motion abnormalities[152](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-152).

### Dobutamine protocol

- Exercise is the preferred stress for a patient who can exercise, because the test also measures the exercise capacity. Dobutamine is the alternative for a patient who cannot exercise, and the preferred agent for the assessment of viability[152](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-152).
- Dobutamine starts at 5 µg/kg/min and increases every 3 minutes to 10, 20, 30 and 40 µg/kg/min. When the heart rate does not reach its target, atropine is given in doses of 0.25 to 0.5 mg at 1-minute intervals, to a total of 1.0 to 2.0 mg[152](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-152).
- The target heart rate is 85% of the age-predicted maximum[152](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-152). The maximum is commonly estimated as 220 − age, as in the figure: for its 60-year-old patient, 160/min, and the target is 136/min. In a meta-analysis of 351 studies (18,712 subjects), 208 − 0.7 × age predicted the maximum better, and 220 − age underestimates it in older adults[154](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-154): for this patient, 166/min, and a target of 141/min. A test that does not reach the target heart rate is less sensitive for ischemia[152](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-152).
- The test stops at the target heart rate, or for hypotension, new or worsening wall motion abnormalities, significant arrhythmias, severe hypertension or intolerable symptoms[152](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-152).
- Stress testing is contraindicated in acute coronary syndromes, severe arrhythmias, malignant hypertension, significant LVOT obstruction and symptomatic severe aortic stenosis[152](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-152).

### Images

The images of each stage are recorded digitally. The peak images are compared with the rest, low-dose and pre-peak or early recovery images, side by side in a quad-screen format[152](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-152). The figure shows rest, low dose, peak and early recovery, as some laboratories do[152](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-152). Each of its views beats at the heart rate of its stage; systole shortens with the heart rate as total electromechanical systole does at rest[155](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-155), and diastole shortens more. The LVEF and the end-systolic volume in the readouts are measured on the figure's own four- and two-chamber views, by the biplane method of disks, as in [chapter 08](https://intro2echo.com/eject). The patient and the numbers are illustrative.

An ultrasound enhancing agent (contrast) is used whenever two or more contiguous segments, or a coronary territory, cannot be visualized[152](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-152).

### Reading the test

- Each segment is scored at rest and at each stage: 1, normal or hyperkinetic; 2, hypokinetic; 3, severely hypokinetic or akinetic; 4, dyskinetic; 5, aneurysmal. The wall motion score index is the sum of the scores divided by the number of segments visualized[152](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-152). The segments and their coronary territories are those of [chapter 10](https://intro2echo.com/segments).
- **Normal.** Every segment is normal at rest and with stress. With dobutamine, the normal response is a marked increase in contractility (hyperkinesis) and a smaller LV cavity, from the lower preload and afterload. The absence of hyperkinesis can indicate coronary obstruction, but it is not specific[152](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-152).
- **Ischemic.** A new wall motion abnormality with stress in a segment that is normal at rest, or worse function with stress in a segment that is abnormal at rest[152](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-152).
- **Fixed.** A severe abnormality at rest that does not change with stress, with no biphasic response: a transmural infarct, or one with a limited epicardial rim of viable myocardium. Akinesis that turns into dyskinesis is a mechanical response of infarcted myocardium, not ischemia[152](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-152).
- **Viable.** A dysfunctional segment that improves at low dose has contractile reserve. A biphasic response, improvement at low dose and worsening at higher doses, has the highest positive predictive value for recovery of function after revascularization; segments with sustained improvement are less likely to recover. Remodeled segments, supplied by arteries without severe stenosis, may or may not improve with dobutamine[152](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-152). Contractile reserve in 4 to 5 dysfunctional segments is the minimum that predicts an increase in LVEF of 5% or more and a better outcome with revascularization[152](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-152).

### High-risk findings and prognosis

- Stress-induced wall motion abnormalities in more than one coronary territory, and cavity dilatation, are high-risk findings that the report should note. With dobutamine, a fall in LVEF and a larger end-systolic cavity are less frequent than with exercise, even in left main or multivessel disease[152](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-152).
- Wall motion abnormalities that occur at a low heart rate or rate–pressure product usually indicate severe stenosis or multivessel disease. Abnormalities that persist into recovery indicate more severe ischemia[152](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-152).
- In a study of 1,500 patients, the peak wall motion score index separated three groups: an index of 1, with a cardiac event rate of 0.9% a year; an index of 1.1 to 1.7, with 3.1% a year; and an index of 1.7 or more, with 5.2% a year[152](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-152).
- The earlier ASE guideline reported an average sensitivity of 88% and an average specificity of 83% for coronary stenosis. As with every stress test, the sensitivity is higher in multivessel than in single-vessel disease[152](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-152).

### Other uses

Stress echocardiography also answers questions outside coronary disease[156](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-156). In classical low-flow, low-gradient aortic stenosis, low-dose dobutamine tests the flow reserve, an increase in stroke volume of 20% or more ( [chapter 12](https://intro2echo.com/as))[156](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-156). With exercise, an average E/e′ above 14 or a septal E/e′ above 15 indicates a raised filling pressure ( [chapter 09](https://intro2echo.com/fill))[152](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-152). In valve disease and in hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, exercise shows the symptoms, the PA systolic pressure and the LVOT gradient; a PA systolic pressure of 60 mmHg or more and an LVOT gradient above 50 mmHg are cut-offs of clinical significance[156](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-156).

### Key points

1. Stress echocardiography compares each segment at rest and with stress. A new or worse wall motion abnormality indicates ischemia.
2. Dobutamine increases in 3-minute stages from 5 to 40 µg/kg/min, with atropine if needed, until the heart rate reaches 85% of the age-predicted maximum.
3. Abnormalities in more than one territory, at a low heart rate or that persist into recovery indicate severe or multivessel disease.
4. A dysfunctional segment that improves at low dose is viable. A biphasic response best predicts recovery of function after revascularization.

B10

## Complications of myocardial infarction

Septal rupture, papillary muscle rupture, free-wall rupture and LV aneurysm

An infarcted wall can tear, through the septum, a papillary muscle or the free wall, or it can stretch into an aneurysm. Echocardiography shows each lesion and measures its effect on flow and pressure.

Stage Qp/Qs ≥1.5

no rupturesmallQp/Qs ≥1.5RVSP ≥50

Complication

Septal rupturePapillary muscleFree wallAneurysm

Rupture11mm

Qp/Qs2.0

Peak velocity3.8m/s

LV–RV gradient58mmHg

Systolic BP103mmHg

RV systolic pressure45mmHg

**Ventricular septal rupture with a significant shunt.** An 11-mm defect in the akinetic apical septum carries left-to-right flow in systole[159](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-159). Qp/Qs is (2.3² × 27) ÷ (2.0² × 18) = 2.0, 1.5 or more: a hemodynamically significant shunt[162](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-162). CW Doppler across the rupture peaks at 3.8 m/s, an LV–RV gradient of 58 mmHg. With a systolic blood pressure of 103 mmHg, the RV systolic pressure is 103 − 58 = 45 mmHg (mildly raised)[160](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-160), [32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32). The RV takes a volume load[161](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-161).

### Mechanical complications

A transmural infarction can rupture the septum, a papillary muscle or the free wall, typically 3 to 5 days after the infarction. Most of these mechanical complications are surgical emergencies, and echocardiography shows each of them[157](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-157). A scar that thins forms an aneurysm[158](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-158). Each lesion lies in the territory of the infarct ( [chapter 10](https://intro2echo.com/segments)).

### Ventricular septal rupture

A septal rupture occurs in fewer than 0.3% of patients in the era of routine primary revascularization, and without repair the mortality approaches 80% at 30 days[157](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-157). Anterior and apical ruptures follow infarcts in the LAD territory, and posterior ruptures follow inferior infarcts, which are associated with complex ruptures with multiple, irregular connections[157](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-157).

Color Doppler shows the left-to-right flow across the septum: it found all 12 confirmed ruptures, and two-dimensional criteria found 7[159](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-159). The peak velocity of the jet on CW gives the LV–RV gradient as 4V², and the systolic blood pressure minus that gradient is the RV systolic pressure[160](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-160). [Bonus chapter B8](https://intro2echo.com/shunts) applies the same relation to a congenital VSD and measures Qp/Qs from the two outflow tracts.

The shunt overloads the RV with volume, raises the pulmonary blood flow and, in turn, overloads the LA and the LV[161](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-161). A shunt is hemodynamically significant when Qp/Qs is 1.5 or more, or when a chamber downstream of it enlarges[162](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-162); after a septal rupture, Qp/Qs reaches 8:1 at catheterization, depending on the size of the defect[157](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-157). In the figure the rupture grows to 18 mm; in one series the rupture measured 13.9 ± 4.4 mm by echocardiography[163](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-163).

### Papillary muscle rupture

Papillary muscle rupture complicates 0.05% to 0.26% of acute infarctions in the reperfusion era. The anterolateral muscle has a dual blood supply; the posteromedial muscle has a single supply, from the circumflex or the right coronary artery. So anterolateral rupture is extremely uncommon, and posteromedial rupture typically follows an inferior or lateral infarction[157](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-157). The posteromedial muscle most commonly has two heads[164](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-164), and the rupture may be partial or complete[157](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-157).

The posteromedial muscle holds the medial half of both leaflets[165](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-165). Its rupture can disrupt either leaflet[166](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-166). A flail leaflet has lost its attachment to the LV, and its tip points toward the roof of the LA[165](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-165); the torn head is a mobile mass in the LV that sometimes prolapses into the LA[157](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-157). In operated patients, transthoracic echocardiography made the diagnosis in 53.8%, and a flail leaflet was seen in every complete rupture and in 40% of partial ones[167](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-167); when the transthoracic study is not diagnostic, TEE has a high sensitivity[157](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-157).

A flail leaflet or a ruptured papillary muscle is specific for severe MR. In acute severe MR, a low blood pressure and a high LA pressure lower the driving pressure, so the jet is slow and color may show little turbulence. The jet is often eccentric, and an eccentric jet looks smaller than a central jet of the same severity. A hyperdynamic LV with a low forward flow supports the diagnosis, the LV and the LA can be of normal size, and the pulmonary veins usually show systolic flow reversal. The Doppler grades of chronic MR ( [chapter 15](https://intro2echo.com/mr)) do not apply[29](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-29).

A triangular CW contour with an early peak shows a high LA pressure or a large v wave, but it is not sensitive for severe MR[29](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-29). The systolic blood pressure minus the peak MR gradient estimates the LA pressure[168](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-168).

### Free-wall rupture and pseudoaneurysm

Free-wall rupture usually causes sudden death. It presents with tamponade and shock, and echocardiography shows the tamponade and sometimes flow across the defect[157](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-157). [Bonus chapter B5](https://intro2echo.com/peri) grades the effusion and the signs of tamponade.

When adherent pericardium contains the rupture, a pseudoaneurysm forms; a true aneurysm, in contrast, is a thinned scar[158](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-158). Pseudoaneurysms more often involve the inferior or the lateral wall and usually have a narrow neck[157](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-157). In pseudoaneurysms the width of the neck was at most 0.5 of the largest parallel width of the sac, and the sac extended behind the intact wall; in true aneurysms the ratio was 0.9 to 1.0[169](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-169).

The wall of the sac is thrombus or pericardium without myocardium. Red flags on imaging include a narrow neck and a break in the myocardium. Doppler shows turbulent flow across the neck, and an enhancing agent helps to show the sac; in published cases, echocardiography missed 18.6% of the pseudoaneurysms[170](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-170). A pseudoaneurysm is prone to rupture[171](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-171), and it is a surgical emergency[157](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-157). An effusion that holds clot is hard to assess by TTE; TEE, CT or CMR assess it better[128](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-128).

### True aneurysm and thrombus

An aneurysm is a focal dilatation with thinning and an akinetic or dyskinetic wall; the ASE scores it as akinetic or dyskinetic, with no separate score[14](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-14). Its wall is thin scar, most often at the anterior wall or the apex after a total occlusion of the LAD, and it is associated with angina, thrombus, worsening heart failure and ventricular tachyarrhythmia[157](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-157). The mouth of a true aneurysm is wide, nearly as wide as the aneurysm itself[169](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-169).

A thrombus lies in up to 50% of chronic LV aneurysms, and it embolizes less often there, probably because the aneurysm does not contract[44](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-44). [Bonus chapter B9](https://intro2echo.com/masses) shows the types of LV thrombus and the enhancing agent.

### RV infarction

The more proximal a right coronary occlusion, the more RV myocardium the infarct involves[33](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-33), and RV-predominant shock follows a right coronary infarct that involves the RV[157](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-157). McConnell's sign occurs in RV infarction as often as in pulmonary embolism[172](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-172), and S′ assumes that one segment represents the whole RV, which is not likely in a regional disease such as RV infarction[33](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-33). [Chapter 11](https://intro2echo.com/rv) measures the size and the function of the RV.

### Key points

1. Color Doppler shows a septal rupture's left-to-right flow; the RV systolic pressure is the systolic blood pressure minus 4V² of the jet.
2. Papillary muscle rupture usually involves the posteromedial muscle, after an inferior or lateral infarction. The flail leaflet makes the MR severe, even when the jet looks small and slow.
3. A free-wall rupture bleeds into the pericardium and causes tamponade. A contained rupture forms a pseudoaneurysm, with a neck at most 0.5 of the sac's width.
4. A true aneurysm has a wide mouth and a wall of thin scar; up to 50% of chronic aneurysms hold a thrombus.

B8

## Shunts: ASD, VSD and PFO

Septal defects, Qp/Qs and the saline study

A septal defect or a patent foramen ovale lets blood pass between the left and the right heart. Echocardiography shows the defect and its flow, measures Qp/Qs, and grades its effect on the chambers and the pulmonary pressure. Across a restrictive atrial communication, the shunt velocity gives the LA pressure.

Shunt significant ASD

nonesmallsignificant

Defect

ASDVSDPFOLA pressure

Saline study

RestValsalva

ASD diameter13mm

Shuntleft to right

Qp/Qs1.8

RV basal diameter4.3cm

RA area20cm²

RV systolic pressure28mmHg

**Hemodynamically significant secundum ASD.** The defect measures 13 mm in the subcostal view, and the shunt is left to right. Qp/Qs is (2.4² × 25) ÷ (2.0² × 20) = 1.8, 1.5 or more. The RV basal diameter is 4.3 cm (mildly dilated) and the RA area 20 cm² (enlarged)[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32). RV volume overload is the key finding of a relevant shunt[126](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-126). The peak TR velocity is 2.5 m/s, so the RV systolic pressure is 25 + 3 = 28 mmHg (normal)[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32).

### Atrial septal defect

Secundum defects, at the fossa ovalis, make up 80% of ASDs; sinus venosus defects in general require TEE[126](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-126). The shunt is in general relevant from a defect size of 10 mm[126](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-126). RV volume overload is the key finding, and it characterizes the hemodynamic relevance of the defect better than the shunt ratio[126](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-126). The RA and the RV dilate, and the ventricular septum flattens in diastole[173](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-173) ( [chapter 11](https://intro2echo.com/rv)).

The subcostal four-chamber view is preferred: the septum lies nearly perpendicular to the beam, so a true defect is told from dropout and measured[173](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-173). In the apical four-chamber view the septum lies parallel to the beam, so an ASD should not be diagnosed or measured there; that view shows the RA and RV dilation and gives the RV pressure from the TR jet[173](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-173). The diameter is measured without color, which can overestimate the defect, and the color scale is lowered to 25–40 cm/s for the low-velocity shunt flow[173](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-173).

### Ventricular septal defect

Perimembranous defects, next to the tricuspid and aortic valves, make up 80% of VSDs[126](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-126). CW Doppler through the defect gives the LV–RV gradient as 4V², and the RV systolic pressure is the LV or aortic systolic pressure minus that gradient[174](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-174). The figure takes a systolic blood pressure of 125 mmHg for the aortic systolic pressure.

A small VSD with an insignificant left-to-right shunt, no LV volume overload and no pulmonary hypertension is restrictive[126](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-126), and its jet is fast[175](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-175). A large shunt loads the LA and the LV with volume[126](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-126). The figure calls the LV enlarged above an LVEDD of 58 mm and the LA above a volume index of 34 mL/m², the upper limits of normal for men[14](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-14). Aortic regurgitation from cusp prolapse should be sought, especially with outlet and high perimembranous defects[126](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-126).

### Qp/Qs and closure

Qp/Qs is the RVOT stroke volume ÷ the LVOT stroke volume[173](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-173) ( [chapter 07](https://intro2echo.com/pressure)), and the calculation can have an error of up to 20%[9](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-9). A shunt is hemodynamically significant when the chambers distal to it are enlarged and/or Qp:Qs is 1.5:1 or more; otherwise it is small or trivial[162](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-162). The figure takes the RV basal diameter as the chamber for an ASD, and the LVEDD for a VSD.

The AHA/ACC guideline recommends closure of an isolated secundum ASD that impairs functional capacity, with RA and/or RV enlargement and a net left-to-right shunt with physiological sequelae (for example Qp:Qs of 1.5:1 or more) without cyanosis; in an adult without symptoms but with these findings, closure is reasonable[162](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-162). It recommends closure of a VSD with LV volume overload and a Qp:Qs of 1.5:1 or more. For both, the condition is a systolic PA pressure below 50% of the systemic pressure and a pulmonary vascular resistance below one third of the systemic resistance; with a VSD and a systolic PA pressure of 50% or more, closure may be considered[162](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-162). An ASD should not be closed with a systolic PA pressure or a pulmonary vascular resistance above two thirds of the systemic value, and/or a net right-to-left shunt[162](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-162). In an ASD, a calculated systolic PA pressure above 40 mmHg requires catheterization to measure the pulmonary vascular resistance[126](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-126).

### Patent foramen ovale and the saline study

Agitated saline forms microbubbles too large to pass the normal pulmonary vessels, so bubbles in the left heart have crossed a shunt[173](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-173), [4](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-4). Bubbles in the LA within 3–6 beats of the first beat with the RA and the RV fully opacified[4](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-4) indicate an intracardiac shunt such as a PFO; later bubbles, from the pulmonary veins, indicate an intrapulmonary shunt such as an arteriovenous malformation[173](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-173).

A Valsalva maneuver or a cough is usually needed to show a PFO that does not shunt at rest[173](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-173). The strain is released when the contrast arrives in the RA[4](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-4); a shift of the atrial septum toward the LA at the release shows that the RA pressure exceeded the LA pressure[173](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-173). An inadequate Valsalva maneuver or RA opacification gives false negatives, and TEE is the reference standard[173](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-173). The number of bubbles in one frame depends on the contrast injected and the maneuver[173](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-173). In patients aged 18 to 60 years with a prior PFO-associated stroke, the SCAI guideline recommends PFO closure rather than antiplatelet therapy alone[176](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-176).

### LA pressure from an atrial shunt

A restrictive atrial communication keeps a pressure difference between the atria. Across it, CW Doppler gives the LA–RA gradient as 4V², so the LA pressure is the RA pressure + 4V²[177](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-177), [178](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-178). After transseptal mitral edge-to-edge repair, the mean gradient + the central venous pressure gave the mean LA pressure[177](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-177), and the peak gradient + the RA pressure gave the peak LA pressure, with limits of agreement of −6.2 to +3.1 mmHg[178](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-178). Both studies used TEE and a catheter RA pressure[177](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-177), [178](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-178). On TTE the RA pressure comes from the IVC ( [chapter 07](https://intro2echo.com/pressure)), and its error adds directly to the LA pressure.

A large ASD lets the atrial pressures equalize: in ASD the mean transatrial septal velocity was 41 ± 11 cm/s[179](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-179), a gradient of about 1 mmHg, so the shunt cannot show a raised LA pressure. A PFO can shunt left to right when a left-sided lesion raises the LA pressure: in one series, every PFO that did had an LA pressure above 13 mmHg and an LA–RA gradient above 10 mmHg[180](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-180).

The figure takes a 5-mm communication and an RA pressure of 3 mmHg, from a normal IVC, and its slider sets the mean LA pressure; the pressure waveforms are teaching values. It calls a mean LA pressure above 15 mmHg raised, the cut-off for an abnormally elevated LV filling pressure in the study that validated the algorithm of [chapter 09](https://intro2echo.com/fill) [43](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-43).

### Key points

1. An ASD is measured in the subcostal four-chamber view. A shunt is hemodynamically significant when it enlarges the chambers distal to it and/or Qp:Qs is 1.5 or more.
2. Across a VSD, the RV systolic pressure is the systemic systolic pressure minus 4V² of the jet.
3. On the saline study, LA bubbles within 3–6 beats of full RA opacification indicate an intracardiac shunt such as a PFO; Valsalva release can show a shunt absent at rest.
4. Across a restrictive atrial communication, the LA pressure is the RA pressure + 4V²; across a large ASD the atrial pressures equalize.

11

## Right ventricle

RV size and function, pressure and volume overload, acute pulmonary embolism

The RV is assessed by its size, its systolic function and the pressure that it generates. The septum flattens at end-systole in pressure overload and at end-diastole only in volume overload; in acute pulmonary embolism the RV dilates while its wall stays thin.

Severity moderate PH

normalmildmoderatesevere

Overload

PressureVolumeAcute PE

Frame

CineEnd-diastoleEnd-systole

RV basal diameter4.1cm

RV free wall7.5mm

TAPSE18mm

RV systolic pressure57mmHg

LVEI, ED / ES1.10 / 1.30

**Moderate pulmonary hypertension.** The peak TR velocity is 3.5 m/s and the estimated RA pressure 8 mmHg, so the RV systolic pressure is 57 mmHg, the range of moderate PH (50–69 mmHg)[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32). The RV basal diameter is 4.1 cm (mildly dilated), the RV free wall 7.5 mm (moderately thick) and the RA area 19 cm² (mildly enlarged). TAPSE is 18 mm (normal), S′ 11.0 cm/s (normal) and FAC 31% (mildly reduced)[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32). The LV eccentricity index is 1.10 at end-diastole and 1.30 at end-systole: the septum is flat at end-systole, the pattern of RV pressure overload[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32). TAPSE and S′ measure the base of the free wall, which can keep its motion in a pressure-overloaded RV, so they can overestimate RV function while FAC falls[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32).

### RV size

The RV is measured at end-diastole in the RV-focused apical four-chamber view; at least one measure of RV size, typically the basal diameter just beneath the tricuspid annulus, should be reported[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32). In the four-chamber view, the RV is normally no more than two-thirds the size of the LV, and the LV forms the apex[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32).

- The RV basal diameter is normal below 4.1 cm, mildly dilated at 4.1–4.4 cm, moderately at 4.5–4.9 cm and severely above 4.9 cm[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32).
- The RV free wall is normally thinner than 5 mm at end-diastole; a thick wall is not specific, as it occurs in pulmonary hypertension and in infiltrative and inherited cardiomyopathies[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32).
- The RV area is normal below 25 cm² at end-diastole and below 14 cm² at end-systole[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32).
- The RA area at end-systole is normal below 19 cm²[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32).

### RV systolic function

The ASE guideline grades these measures of RV systolic function[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32):

- **TAPSE.** TAPSE is the M-mode excursion of the lateral tricuspid annulus toward the apex[4](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-4). It is normal above 17 mm.
- **S′.** S′ is the systolic tissue Doppler velocity of the same point. It is normal above 9.5 cm/s.
- **Fractional area change.** The fractional area change (FAC) is (end-diastolic area − end-systolic area) ÷ end-diastolic area. It is normal above 35%[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32).
- **Free-wall strain.** The longitudinal strain of the RV free wall is normal when it is more negative than −20%[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32).

TAPSE and S′ measure the motion of one point of the annulus; FAC comes from the whole RV area in one plane, and so includes the radial motion of the free wall and the septum[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32). In a pressure-overloaded RV that has begun to fail, the base often keeps its motion, so TAPSE and S′ can overestimate RV function[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32).

### Pressure overload

The RV systolic pressure is 4 × the peak TR velocity² + the RA pressure ( [chapter 07](https://intro2echo.com/pressure)), which comes from the IVC ( [chapter 07](https://intro2echo.com/pressure)); it equals the PA systolic pressure when there is no RVOT obstruction or pulmonic stenosis[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32). The ASE guideline grades it as normal up to 34 mmHg, mildly raised at 35–49 mmHg, moderately at 50–69 mmHg and severely at 70 mmHg or more[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32).

As the resistance of the pulmonary vessels rises, the RVOT acceleration time, normally above 105 ms, shortens, and the RVOT flow can notch in mid-systole, the W sign[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32).

The RV first adapts with concentric hypertrophy and a preserved ejection fraction. When it maladapts, it dilates, its function falls and its shape changes from a crescent to a sphere; the tricuspid annulus can then dilate, with functional TR[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32).

### Volume overload

Significant TR or PR, an atrial septal defect and anomalous pulmonary venous drainage put a volume load on the RV, and the right heart dilates[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32). In severe TR, the TR velocity can underestimate the RV–RA gradient[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32) ( [chapter 16](https://intro2echo.com/tr)).

### The septum

The LV eccentricity index (LVEI) is the ratio of two diameters of the LV in the short axis at the papillary muscles: D2, parallel to the septum, over D1, from the septum across the LV[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32). In a normal heart the LV is round, and the index is about 1 at end-diastole and at end-systole[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32), [181](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-181).

- An index above 1 at end-diastole only suggests RV volume overload.
- An index above 1 at end-systole suggests RV pressure overload.
- An index above 1 at both times suggests RV pressure overload, with or without volume overload[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32).

Conduction abnormalities, pacing, cardiac surgery and constriction can change the motion of the septum[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32).

### Acute pulmonary embolism

Acute pulmonary embolism puts a sudden pressure load on an RV that has not hypertrophied: it enlarges before the pulmonary pressures rise, and its wall thickens only later[33](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-33). The ESC guideline shows the signs of RV pressure overload: a basal RV/LV diameter ratio above 1.0, a flattened septum, a distended IVC with less inspiratory collapse, a TAPSE below 16 mm, an S′ below 9.5 cm/s, the 60/60 sign, McConnell's sign and a mobile thrombus in the right heart[182](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-182).

McConnell's sign is akinesia of the mid RV free wall with normal motion at the apex; in the first series, the mid free wall moved −0.2 ± 0.8 mm[183](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-183). The 60/60 sign is a pulmonary ejection acceleration time, measured in the RVOT, below 60 ms with a peak systolic TR gradient below 60 mmHg[182](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-182). Both signs are specific but insensitive[184](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-184); pooled over 11 studies, McConnell's sign had a sensitivity of 29.1% and a specificity of 98.6%[185](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-185). McConnell's sign also occurs in RV infarction[172](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-172) ( [bonus chapter B10](https://intro2echo.com/mi)); signs of RV pressure overload help to distinguish acute PE from RV infarction[182](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-182).

A peak TR velocity above 3.8 m/s, a TR gradient above 60 mmHg or a thick RV wall goes beyond what an acute pressure load produces, and chronic thromboembolic or other pulmonary hypertension should then be considered[182](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-182). The figure's pressure load is that chronic picture: its RV wall thickens to 9.5 mm and its TR velocity reaches 4.4 m/s on the normal heart, against 4.0 mm and 3.3 m/s or less under acute PE.

Echocardiography is not mandatory in a hemodynamically stable patient with suspected PE, and a normal study does not exclude PE: its negative predictive value is 40–50%[182](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-182). In suspected high-risk PE with hemodynamic instability, the absence of RV overload or dysfunction practically excludes PE as the cause. Unequivocal RV pressure overload with no other obvious cause justifies emergency reperfusion when CT angiography is not feasible, in a patient with a high clinical probability[182](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-182). An RV/LV ratio of 1.0 or more and a TAPSE below 16 mm are the findings most often linked to an unfavorable prognosis[182](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-182); the grades of the figure are echocardiographic findings, not risk classes.

### The probability of pulmonary hypertension

Pulmonary hypertension is defined by a mean PA pressure above 20 mmHg at rest. Echocardiography gives a probability of PH. The ESC/ERS guideline uses the peak TR velocity, not the estimated PA systolic pressure, because the estimate of the RA pressure adds error:

- A peak TR velocity of 2.8 m/s or less, or no measurable velocity, gives a low probability, or an intermediate one with other signs.
- A velocity of 2.9–3.4 m/s gives an intermediate probability, or a high one with other signs.
- A velocity above 3.4 m/s gives a high probability.

The other signs are in three categories. The ventricles: an RV/LV basal ratio above 1.0, a flat septum with an LVEI above 1.1, and a TAPSE/sPAP ratio below 0.55 mm/mmHg. The pulmonary artery: an RVOT acceleration time below 105 ms or mid-systolic notching, an early-diastolic PR velocity above 2.2 m/s, and a PA wider than the aortic root or wider than 25 mm. The IVC and the RA: an IVC wider than 21 mm with a reduced collapse, and an RA area above 18 cm². Signs change the probability only when they come from at least two categories. The ASE guideline calls a TR velocity of 2.9 m/s or more, or 2.8 m/s with at least two other signs, suggestive of PH.

The signs are not specific to a pressure load. In the figure, a volume load reaches an intermediate probability with a normal TR velocity, through the RV/LV ratio, the septum at end-diastole, the PA diameter and the RA area.

### Key points

1. At least one measure each of RV size, typically the basal diameter[32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32), and of RV function should be reported[36](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-36).
2. The septum flattens at end-systole in pressure overload and at end-diastole only in volume overload.
3. The probability of PH comes from the peak TR velocity and from signs in at least two of three categories: the ventricles, the pulmonary artery, and the IVC and the RA.
4. In a failing, pressure-overloaded RV, TAPSE and S′ can overestimate RV function; FAC also shows the radial motion of the free wall and the septum.
5. In acute pulmonary embolism the RV dilates while its wall stays thin and the TR gradient stays at 60 mmHg or less. McConnell's sign and the 60/60 sign are specific but insensitive, and a normal echocardiogram does not exclude PE[182](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-182), [184](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-184).

17

## Transesophageal echocardiography

The probe, its movements and the standard views

Transesophageal echocardiography (TEE) images the heart from the esophagus and the stomach, directly behind the left atrium. Each standard view is set by the level of the probe, its turn and the multiplane angle.

Level

Upper esophagealMid-esophagealTransgastric

Turn

LeftNeutralRight

Multiplane angle 5°

4CMC2CLAX

Free probe

Free probe, from the position above

Advance 0.0 cm

Turn 0°

Anteflex 0°

Side flex 0°

Back to the starting position

ViewME four-chamber (Hahn view 2; one of the 11 basic views)

Level and angleMid-esophageal, 5° (guideline 0–10°)

Probe movement (guideline)Advance to the mitral valve; slight retroflexion may be needed to align the mitral valve and the LV apex

ShowsLA, RA, atrial septum, LV, RV, ventricular septum, mitral valve (A3A2 and P2P1), tricuspid valve

**ME four-chamber.** At this angle the patient’s right is on the left of the display. Views, angles and probe movements as in the ASE/SCA guideline[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90); the basic examination of 11 views[186](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-186).

### The probe and its movements

The probe tip is advanced into the esophagus or the stomach, or withdrawn[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90). Rotating the probe clockwise, toward the patient's right, is turning to the right; rotating it counterclockwise is turning to the left[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90). The large control wheel flexes the tip anteriorly (anteflexion) or posteriorly (retroflexion), and the small wheel to the patient's right or left[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90). Buttons on the handle rotate the imaging plane electronically from 0° to 180°: toward 180° is forward rotation, toward 0° backward rotation[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90). The basic examination uses three positions: the upper esophageal (UE), the mid-esophageal (ME) and the transgastric (TG) level[186](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-186). The transthoracic views are in [chapter 04](https://intro2echo.com/windows).

### Image orientation

The transducer is at the top of the image, with the near field close to it[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90). At 0°, the transverse plane, the patient's right appears on the left of the display[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90). Forward rotation to 90°, the longitudinal plane, moves the left of the display inferiorly[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90). At 180°, the image is the mirror image of 0°[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90). From the mid-esophagus, the LA lies in the near field. An icon on the screen shows the angle[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90).

### The standard views

The ASE/SCA guideline describes 28 views of a comprehensive examination, each with its level, approximate angle, probe movement from the previous view and the structures imaged[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90). Flexion or turning may be needed in a given patient[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90). The basic perioperative examination uses 11 of the views and starts in the ME four-chamber view[186](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-186). The figure shows 17 views, including all 11 basic views.

- **Mid-esophageal, neutral.** From the four-chamber view at 0–10°, forward rotation gives the mitral commissural view at 50–70°, the two-chamber view at 80–100° and the long-axis view at 120–140°[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90). The long-axis view has the orientation of the transthoracic long-axis or three-chamber view[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90).
- **Mid-esophageal, turned right.** In the aortic valve short-axis view at 25–45°, the left coronary cusp lies in the near field on the right of the display, the noncoronary cusp next to the atrial septum and the right coronary cusp in the far field[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90). The RV inflow-outflow view lies at 50–70°, the bicaval view at 90–110° and the aortic valve long-axis view at 120–140°[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90).
- **Mid-esophageal, turned left.** From the two-chamber view at 90–110°, turning to the left gives the LA appendage view[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90). Advancing or anteflexing the probe may open the appendage, and the left upper pulmonary vein often shows in the same view[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90).
- **Upper esophageal.** The ascending aorta appears in long axis at 90–110°, with the right pulmonary artery behind it, and in short axis at 0–30°[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90). Table 10 of the guideline gives these two ME views the upper esophageal level[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90). Higher, the aortic arch appears in long axis at 0–10° and in short axis at 70–90°[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90).
- **Transgastric.** The anteflexed probe shows the LV in short axis at the papillary muscles at 0–20°, with myocardium of the left anterior descending, circumflex and right coronary arteries together[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90). At 90–110°, the TG two-chamber view shows the anterior and inferior walls[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90).
- **Descending aorta.** Turned to the left, the probe shows the descending aorta in short axis at 0–10° and in long axis at 90–100°[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90). It lies posterior to the esophagus and to its left[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90).

### What TEE adds to transthoracic imaging

TEE is indicated when its findings will change management and TTE is nondiagnostic, or likely to be[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90). TTE may be nondiagnostic for far-field structures, such as the aorta and the LA appendage, and for prosthetic valves, native valve masses and paravalvular abscesses[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90). The guideline rates TEE as appropriate in suspected acute aortic pathology, including dissection, and in suspected endocarditis with a moderate or high pretest probability[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90). [Chapter 21](https://intro2echo.com/aorta) covers aortic dissection, [bonus chapter B6](https://intro2echo.com/prosthetic) prosthetic valves and [bonus chapter B7](https://intro2echo.com/endocarditis) endocarditis.

The LA appendage is the primary target in the search for a cardiac source of embolus[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90). TEE detects appendage thrombus with a sensitivity of 100% and a specificity of 99%, but thrombi smaller than 2 mm can be missed[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90). In the ACUTE trial, in atrial fibrillation of more than two days, early cardioversion guided by TEE had an embolic rate similar to that of cardioversion after 3 weeks of warfarin, with fewer hemorrhagic events[187](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-187).

In an agitated saline study, more than three bubbles in the left heart within three beats of right heart opacification are most consistent with a patent foramen ovale[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90). [Bonus chapter B8](https://intro2echo.com/shunts) shows the shunts.

### The interactive figure

The left pane shows the heart model from the patient's left, with the esophagus behind the LA, the probe at its level and its imaging plane. The right pane shows the echo image that the plane cuts from the same mesh, in the guideline's orientation. The Level and Turn switches move the probe, and the slider rotates the plane from 0° to 180°; when it stops near the angle of a view, it snaps to that view. Between two views of one level and turn, the probe moves from one view's position to the other's.

The Free probe switch releases the probe. Four sliders then advance or withdraw it along the esophagus, turn it, ante- or retroflex it and flex it to the side. With the probe free, the multiplane angle turns the plane about the beam, and the probe does not move. The panel names the nearest standard view, with the difference in degrees and centimeters. Play shows the views in the guideline's order. After the four-chamber view, it frees the probe and moves each control out and back, then all of them together.

The esophagus runs behind the LA of each heart and to the right of the descending aorta, then through the hiatus to the stomach under the LV. In this model, the probe is retroflexed and flexed to the right to show the four-chamber plane at 5°. The mesh's descending aorta ends a little above the hiatus, so for the aortic views the probe lies behind the LA, where the aorta crosses the whole sector.

### Key points

1. The probe is advanced or withdrawn, turned right or left, and flexed; the imaging plane rotates electronically from 0° to 180°[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90).
2. The transducer is at the top of the image. At 0° the patient's right is on the left of the display, and 180° gives the mirror image[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90).
3. The basic perioperative examination uses 11 views and starts in the ME four-chamber view[186](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-186).
4. TEE shows structures that TTE may not: the LA appendage, the aorta, prosthetic valves and abscesses[90](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-90).

19

## Report

Standardized echocardiography reporting

The standardized echocardiography report presents the findings of a study in a fixed order, so that the referring clinician can decide the next step. The summary statement is placed first. The findings for each structure follow, with structure described before function, and every measured value is classified as normal or abnormal.

Case

NormalHFrEF + MRAortic stenosisHFpEFAmyloidIschemic CMBarlow MRBicuspid AS

Sex (sets the normal ranges)

FemaleMale

| Measurement | Value |
| --- | --- |

Derived values used in the report

Normal values

### Order of the report

1. Demographic data, the essential clinical history, the indication for the study and its priority are stated at the top of the report.
2. The summary statement follows and is included in every report.
3. The findings are then reported under a separate heading for each structure: the LV, ventricular septum, RV, LA, atrial septum, RA, the four cardiac valves, aorta, pulmonary artery, IVC, SVC and pericardium.
4. Under each heading, the structure is described before its function, and the Doppler findings are reported with the structure to which they apply.

### Summary statement

The summary statement contains five elements: LV and RV function; significant valve disease, or its absence; other important positive findings; pertinent negative findings for the indication; and a comparison with prior studies. The summary statement should be related to the indication for the study, so that the referring clinician can decide the next step in management. Critical findings should be labeled as critical and listed first, and the person who received them should be recorded.

### Terminology and numerical values

- Each structure and each measured value should be described as normal or abnormal. A severity grade should be assigned only where a guideline defines one.
- Regurgitation that is less than mild should be described as “trace”. The term “mild-to-moderate” should be avoided unless the reason for its use is stated.
- A single LVEF value should be reported, together with the method by which it was measured.
- Pressures, volumes and EF should be reported as whole numbers, and velocities, areas and lengths to the nearest 0.1.
- The descriptive terms must agree with the measured values; for example, “normal LA size” with an LA volume index of 50 mL/m² is an error.
- Image quality should be stated as excellent, good, fair, poor or non-diagnostic. Blood pressure should be measured at the bedside at the start of the study.

### Accreditation standards

The IAC requires that all reports within a laboratory be standardized. Routine inpatient studies must be interpreted within 24 hours, outpatient studies by the end of the next business day, and STAT findings immediately. Every structure that is not adequately visualized must be noted in the report, and the text must agree with the measured values.

### Simulated cases

The four simulated cases are computed with the same models as the figures in the other chapters. Selecting the other sex changes the normal ranges and can change the descriptive terms in the report. In the HFpEF case, an LV mass index of 100 g/m² is classified as LV hypertrophy in a woman and as normal in a man.

### Key points

1. The summary statement is placed at the top of the report, after the demographic data and the indication, and contains five elements.
2. Under each structure heading, the structural findings are described before the functional findings.
3. A single LVEF is reported with its method of measurement, and the descriptive terms must agree with the measured values.

## Sources

Close

Each guideline value on this page is cited to its source. The cut-off values are taken from the American Society of Echocardiography (ASE), the European Association of Cardiovascular Imaging (EACVI), the British Society of Echocardiography (BSE), the ACC/AHA, the ESC and the ESC/ERS.

The figures are generated by simple models calibrated to agree with these sources. The diastolic values and the Doppler and strain values of the amyloid heart are illustrative and are not patient data. All images are simulated, including the drawings, the heart model and the echocardiographic views.

## Normal values

Close

| Interval | Mean ± SD | 95% range |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Isovolumic contraction time | 40 ± 10 ms | 20–59 ms |
| LV ejection time | 292 ± 23 ms | 248–336 ms |
| Isovolumic relaxation time | 96 ± 19 ms | 59–134 ms |

Cardiac time intervals (healthy adults)

Copenhagen City Heart Study: 1,969 adults, color tissue Doppler M-mode. IVRT lengthens with age. [13](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-13)

| Measure | Normal |
| --- | --- |
| Right atrial pressure, mean | 2–6 mmHg |
| Pulmonary artery pressure, systolic | 15–30 mmHg |
| Pulmonary artery pressure, diastolic | 4–12 mmHg |
| Pulmonary artery pressure, mean | 8–20 mmHg |
| Pulmonary artery wedge pressure, mean | ≤12 mmHg |
| Cardiac output | 4–8 L/min |
| Cardiac index | 2.5–4.0 L/min/m² |
| Stroke volume | 60–100 mL |

Resting pressures and flow (catheter)

Pulmonary hypertension is defined by a mean PA pressure above 20 mmHg at rest. The same guideline keeps a wedge pressure of 15 mmHg as the cut-off between pre-capillary and post-capillary pulmonary hypertension. [31](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-31)

| Chamber | Typical (range) |
| --- | --- |
| Left atrium, mean | 8 (2–12) mmHg |
| Left ventricle, peak systolic | 130 (90–140) mmHg |
| Left ventricle, end-diastolic | 9 (5–12) mmHg |
| Right ventricle, peak systolic | 25 (15–30) mmHg |
| Right ventricle, end-diastolic | 4 (0–8) mmHg |

Left heart and right ventricle pressures (textbook)

Adapted from Fowler (1980). These are textbook values, not guideline criteria. [188](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-188)

| IVC diameter and collapse | RA pressure |
| --- | --- |
| ≤2.1 cm and ≥50% collapse | 3 (0–5) mmHg |
| ≤2.1 cm and <50%, or >2.1 cm and ≥50% | 8 (5–10) mmHg |
| >2.1 cm and <50% collapse | 15 (10–20) mmHg |
| >2.5 cm, <50%, dilated hepatic veins, echo contrast or reflux | 20 mmHg |

Right atrial pressure from the IVC

Collapse is assessed with a sniff. A single value, not a range, should be reported. [32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32)

| Measurement | Women | Men |
| --- | --- | --- |
| LVEF, biplane (%) | 54–74 | 52–72 |
| LV end-diastolic volume index (mL/m²) | 29–61 | 34–74 |
| LV end-systolic volume index (mL/m²) | 8–24 | 11–31 |
| LV diameter, end-diastole (mm) | 38–52 | 42–58 |
| LV diameter, end-systole (mm) | 22–35 | 25–40 |
| Septum, end-diastole (mm) | 6–9 | 6–10 |
| Inferolateral wall, end-diastole (mm) | 6–9 | 6–10 |
| LV mass index, linear (g/m²) | 43–95 | 49–115 |
| Relative wall thickness | ≤0.42 | ≤0.42 |
| LA volume index (mL/m²) | 16–34 | 16–34 |

Left ventricle and left atrium (2D)

Values are the mean ± 2 SD of healthy adults. LVEF is mildly abnormal at 41–51% in men and 41–53% in women, moderately abnormal at 30–40% and severely abnormal below 30%. The LA volume index is mildly abnormal at 35–41, moderately at 42–48 and severely above 48 mL/m². [14](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-14)

| Band | GLS |
| --- | --- |
| Normal | more negative than −18% |
| Borderline | −16% to −18% |
| Abnormal | less negative than −16% |

LV global longitudinal strain

On serial studies, a relative change of 10–15% is likely to be significant; the same vendor and software should be used. [39](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-39)

| Category | LVEF |
| --- | --- |
| HFrEF (reduced) | ≤40% |
| HFmrEF (mildly reduced) | 41–49% |
| HFpEF (preserved) | ≥50% |
| HFimpEF (improved) | was ≤40%, now >40% |

Heart failure by LVEF

The diagnosis of HFmrEF or HFpEF also requires evidence of raised filling pressure at rest or during stress. [37](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-37)

| Measurement | Normal | Mild | Moderate | Severe |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| TAPSE (mm) | >17 | ≤17 to >13 | ≤13 to >10 | ≤10 |
| RV S′ (cm/s) | >9.5 | ≤9.5 to >7.2 | ≤7.2 to >5.0 | ≤5.0 |
| Fractional area change (%) | >35 | ≤35 to >29 | ≤29 to >22 | ≤22 |
| RV free-wall strain, absolute (%) | >20 | ≤20 to ≥15 | <15 to ≥11 | <11 |
| RV basal diameter (cm) | <4.1 | 4.1–4.4 | >4.4 to 4.9 | >4.9 |
| RV mid diameter (cm) | <3.5 | 3.5–3.8 | >3.8 to 4.2 | >4.2 |
| RV wall thickness (mm) | <5 | 5–7 | >7 to 9 | >9 |
| RV end-diastolic area (cm²) | <25 | 25–28 | >28 to 32 | >32 |
| RV end-systolic area (cm²) | <14 | 14–16 | >16 to 19 | >19 |
| RA area (cm²) | <19 | 19–22 | >22 to 24 | >24 |
| RA volume index, method of disks (mL/m²) | <30 | 30–36 | >36 to 41 | >41 |
| Peak TR velocity (m/s) | <2.8 | 2.8–3.1 | 3.2–3.5 | ≥3.6 |
| RV systolic pressure (mmHg) | ≤34 | 35–49 | 50–69 | ≥70 |
| RVOT acceleration time (ms) | >105 | 80–105 | 60 to <80 | <60 |
| Main PA diameter (cm) | ≤2.5 | >2.5 to 3.0 | >3.0 to 3.5 | >3.5 |

Right heart

Abnormal denotes mild or worse. A resting peak TR velocity ≥2.9 m/s, or ≥2.8 m/s with two other signs, suggests pulmonary hypertension. [32](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-32)

| Variable | Abnormal if |
| --- | --- |
| e′ septal / lateral / average | ≤6 / ≤7 / ≤6.5 cm/s |
| E/e′ septal / lateral / average | ≥15 / ≥13 / ≥14 |
| Peak TR velocity (if no RA pressure) | ≥2.8 m/s |
| PA systolic pressure (if RA pressure known) | ≥35 mmHg |
| Pulmonary vein S/D | ≤0.67 |
| LA reservoir strain | ≤18% |
| LA volume index | >34 mL/m² |
| IVRT | ≤70 ms |
| E/A, once LA pressure is high | <2 grade 2; ≥2 grade 3 |
| E/A, reduced e′ the only finding | ≤0.8 grade 1 |

LV diastolic function and filling pressure (sinus rhythm)

The algorithm does not apply in atrial fibrillation, mitral stenosis, severe primary MR, moderate or severe annular calcification, mitral repair or replacement, heart transplantation, LVAD support, constriction or non-cardiac pulmonary hypertension. [17](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-17)

| Grade | Vmax (m/s) | Mean gradient (mmHg) | AVA (cm²) | AVA index (cm²/m²) | Velocity ratio |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Sclerosis | ≤2.5 | – | – | – | – |
| Mild | 2.6–2.9 | <20 | >1.5 | >0.85 | >0.50 |
| Moderate | 3.0–4.0 | 20–40 | 1.0–1.5 | 0.60–0.85 | 0.25–0.50 |
| Severe | ≥4.0 | ≥40 | <1.0 | <0.6 | <0.25 |

Aortic stenosis

Any one criterion suggests severe stenosis; concordance of all criteria is preferred. Very severe stenosis is defined by a Vmax ≥5 m/s or a mean gradient ≥60 mmHg. [30](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-30), [38](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-38)

| Grade | MVA (cm²) | Pressure half-time (ms) | Mean gradient (mmHg) | PA systolic (mmHg) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Progressive, mild | >2.5 | <100 | <5 | <30 |
| Progressive, moderate | 1.6–2.5 | 100–149 | 5–9 | 30–49 |
| Severe | ≤1.5 | ≥150 | ≥10 | ≥50 |

Mitral stenosis (rheumatic)

The mean gradient applies at a heart rate of 60–80 bpm. ACC/AHA lists an MVA ≤1.5 cm² and a pressure half-time ≥150 ms for severe stenosis; by MVA = 220 ÷ pressure half-time, 150 ms corresponds to 1.47 cm². [66](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-66), [38](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-38)

| Measure | Mild | Moderate | Severe |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Vena contracta width (cm) | <0.3 | intermediate | ≥0.7 |
| EROA, PISA (cm²) | <0.20 | 0.20–0.39 | ≥0.40 |
| Regurgitant volume (mL) | <30 | 30–59 | ≥60 |
| Regurgitant fraction (%) | <30 | 30–49 | ≥50 |
| PISA radius at Nyquist 30–40 cm/s (cm) | <0.3 | intermediate | ≥1.0 |
| Color jet (Nyquist 50–70 cm/s) | small, central | variable | >50% of LA or wall-hugging |
| CW signal | faint, partial | dense, partial | dense, triangular |
| Pulmonary vein flow | systolic dominant | systolic blunting | systolic reversal |

Mitral regurgitation (chronic)

In secondary MR (crescent-shaped orifice) and in low-flow states, the EROA and the regurgitant volume of severe regurgitation can be lower. [29](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-29)

| Measure | Mild | Moderate | Severe |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Vena contracta width (cm) | <0.3 | 0.3–0.6 | >0.6 |
| Jet width / LVOT width (%) | <25 | 25–64 | ≥65 |
| EROA (cm²) | <0.10 | 0.10–0.29 | ≥0.30 |
| Regurgitant volume (mL) | <30 | 30–59 | ≥60 |
| Regurgitant fraction (%) | <30 | 30–49 | ≥50 |
| Pressure half-time (ms) | >500 | 500–200 | <200 |
| Descending aorta reversal | brief, early | intermediate | holodiastolic |

Aortic regurgitation (chronic)

The pressure half-time shortens as LV diastolic pressure rises. [29](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-29)

| Measure | Mild | Moderate | Severe |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Vena contracta width (cm) | <0.3 | 0.3–0.69 | ≥0.7 |
| EROA, PISA (cm²) | <0.20 | 0.20–0.39 | ≥0.40 |
| Regurgitant volume, PISA (mL) | <30 | 30–44 | ≥45 |
| PISA radius, baseline shift 28 cm/s (cm) | ≤0.5 | 0.6–0.9 | >0.9 |
| Central jet area (cm²) | – | – | >10 |
| Hepatic vein flow | systolic dominant | systolic blunting | systolic reversal |
| IVC diameter (cm) | ≤2.0 | 2.1–2.5 | >2.5 |

Tricuspid regurgitation (chronic)

Grades beyond severe (EACVI): massive, with a vena contracta of 1.4–2.0 cm, and torrential, with a vena contracta ≥2.1 cm. [29](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-29), [65](https://intro2echo.com/#ref-65)
